From now on we focus on two specific projective systems that are related to random matrices. They form the basic integrable structure behind our problem. They come from the study of unitarily invariant measures on infinite Hermitian matrices, see
[
89
,
81
,
16
,
11
]
for background. We will need several definitions and preliminary results. For the most part, the material in this subsection is standard and has appeared in equivalent forms in
[
89
,
81
,
16
,
11
,
8
]
.

For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbb{W}_{N}
be the Weyl chamber,

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{N}=\left\{\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:x_{1}\geq x_{2}\geq\dots\geq x_{N}\right\},

(33)

The interior of
\mathbb{W}_{N}
and
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
from (
5
) will be denoted by
\mathbb{W}_{N}^{\circ}
and
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{\circ}
respectively.

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\mathbb{H}(N)
denote the space of
N\times N
Hermitian matrices, and
\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)\subset\mathbb{H}(N)
the subspace of non-negative definite ones.

Definition 2.11
.

We define the eigenvalue maps

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}_{N}:\mathbb{H}(N)

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{W}_{N},

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{x}=(x_{1}\geq x_{2}\geq\dots\geq x_{N}),

where
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1}\geq x_{2}\geq\dots\geq x_{N})
are the ordered eigenvalues of the matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{H}(N)
. Observe that,
\mathsf{eval}_{N}
restricts to a map from
\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
to
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
.

We now let, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\Pi_{N}^{N+1}:\mathbb{H}(N+1)

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{H}(N),

\displaystyle\left[\mathbf{A}_{ij}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N+1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[\mathbf{A}_{ij}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N},

be the so-called corners map. Also, observe that,
\Pi_{N}^{N+1}
restricts to a map from
\mathbb{H}_{+}(N+1)
to
\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
. Let
\mathbb{U}(N)
be the space of
N\times N
unitary matrices. We will denote by
\mathbf{A}^{\dagger}
the complex conjugate of a matrix
\mathbf{A}
. The following definition is one of our basic building blocks.

Definition 2.12
.

For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{x},\cdot\right)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathsf{Law}\left[\mathsf{eval}_{N}\left(\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{U}\mathsf{diag}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{U}^{{\dagger}}\right)\right)\right],\ \ \forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1},

(34)

where
\mathbf{U}
is Haar-distributed on
\mathbb{U}(N)
and
\mathsf{diag}(\mathbf{x})
indicates the diagonal matrix with entries
\mathbf{x}
. More generally, we define the composition

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K}^{N}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\Lambda_{N-1}^{N}\Lambda_{N-2}^{N-1}\cdots\Lambda_{K}^{K+1},\ \ \forall N>K\geq 1.

(35)

We write
\Delta_{N}
for the Vandermonde determinant given by, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{i}-x_{j}).

When
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
,
\mathbf{y}\prec\mathbf{x}
denotes interlacing:

x_{1}\geq y_{1}\geq\dots\geq y_{N}\geq x_{N+1}
.
It is a classical result by Baryshnikov
[
13
]

(in fact the computation is implicit in
[
42
]
, see also
[
77
]
for an alternative proof) that the following holds. This explicit formula will be useful in the sequel.

Lemma 2.13
.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})=\frac{N!\Delta_{N}(\mathbf{y})}{\Delta_{N+1}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{y}\prec\mathbf{x}\}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y},\ \ \forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1}^{\circ}.

(36)

As expected these Markov kernels are Feller.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
N>K\geq 1
. The Markov kernel
\Lambda_{K}^{N}
is Feller, that is, for all
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{K})
, we have
\Lambda_{K}^{N}f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N})
. Similarly, for all
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{K,+})
, we have
\Lambda_{K}^{N}f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
.

Proof.

The statement follows from Lemma 2.5 of
[
8
]
, and using the fact that the composition of Feller kernels is Feller.
∎

We now define the infinite dimensional space
\Omega
, an analogue of the space
\Omega_{+}
from the introduction, that will allow in the sequel to also consider negative coordinates.

Definition 2.15
.

We define the space
\Omega
as follows:

\displaystyle\Omega\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{\omega=(\mathbf{x}^{+},\mathbf{x}^{-},\gamma,\delta)\in\mathbb{W}_{\infty,+}\times\mathbb{W}_{\infty,+}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}:\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(x_{i}^{+})^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(x_{i}^{-})^{2}\leq\delta\right\},

(37)

endowed with the topology of coordinate-wise convergence.

Observe that,
\Omega
like
\Omega_{+}
is locally compact, metrizable and separable. Its topology can be metrized with the following metric
\mathsf{d}_{\Omega}
, with
\omega=\left(\mathbf{x}^{+},\mathbf{x}^{-},\gamma,\delta\right)

\mathsf{d}_{\Omega}(\omega,\tilde{\omega})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|x_{i}^{+}-\tilde{x}_{i}^{+}\right|}{2^{i}\left(1+\left|x_{i}^{+}-\tilde{x}_{i}^{+}\right|\right)}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|x_{i}^{-}-\tilde{x}_{i}^{-}\right|}{2^{i}\left(1+\left|x_{i}^{-}-\tilde{x}_{i}^{-}\right|\right)}+\left|\gamma-\tilde{\gamma}\right|+\left|\delta-\tilde{\delta}\right|.

(38)

We note moreover that
\Omega_{+}
can be embedded into
\Omega
as follows:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x},\gamma)\mapsto\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0},\gamma,\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}x_{j}^{2}\right),

(39)

and observe that that this is a homeomorphism. This embedding is the natural embedding of
\Omega_{+}
into
\Omega
and this will be clearer in the sequel.

We now embed
\mathbb{W}_{N}
and
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
into
\Omega
and
\Omega_{+}
respectively, in a natural way (again the fact that this is a reasonable thing to do will be clear in what follows).

Definition 2.16
.

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we embed the space
\mathbb{W}_{N}
into
\Omega
as follows, with
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\mapsto\left(\left(N^{-1}\max\left\{x_{i}^{(N)},0\right\}\right)_{i=1}^{\infty},\left(N^{-1}\max\left\{-x_{N+1-i}^{(N)},0\right\}\right)_{i=1}^{\infty},N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{(N)},N^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(x_{i}^{(N)}\right)^{2}\right),

(40)

where by convention
x_{i}^{(N)}\equiv 0
whenever
i\notin\{1,\dots,N\}
. We denote this embedded point by
\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)
.
Similarly, we can define an embedding of
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
into
\Omega_{+}
as follows, with
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\mapsto\left(\left(N^{-1}x_{i}^{(N)}\right)_{i=1}^{\infty},N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{(N)}\right).

(41)

Abusing notation, we denote this embedded point in
\Omega_{+}
by
\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)
again.

Note that, the embedding of
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
into
\Omega_{+}
matches the embedding of the stochastic dynamics from the introduction.

Now, let

\mathbb{H}(\infty)
denotes the space of infinite Hermitian matrices, and write
\mathbb{H}_{+}(\infty)
for the space of non-negative definite infinite Hermitian matrices (defined as the projective limits of the spaces
\left(\mathbb{H}(N)\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
and
\left(\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
respectively under the corners maps
\Pi_{N}^{N+1}
; we do not make this more precise as we will only need to work with their finite-dimensional projections). Define the map,

\displaystyle\Pi_{N}^{\infty}:\mathbb{H}(\infty)

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{H}(N),

\displaystyle\left[\mathbf{A}_{ij}\right]_{i,j=1}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[\mathbf{A}_{ij}\right]_{i,j=1}^{N}.

Observe that this restricts to a map from
\mathbb{H}_{+}(\infty)
to
\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
.

The following definition is important.

Definition 2.17
.

For
\omega\in\Omega
, define the infinite Hermitian random matrix

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\omega}^{\infty}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\gamma\mathbf{I}+\sqrt{\delta-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left[(x_{i}^{+})^{2}+(x_{i}^{-})^{2}\right]}\mathbf{G}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}x_{j}^{+}\left(\boldsymbol{\xi}(j)\boldsymbol{\xi}(j)^{{\dagger}}-\mathbf{I}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}x_{j}^{-}\left(\boldsymbol{\zeta}(j)\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{{\dagger}}(j)-\mathbf{I}\right),

where,
\mathbf{G}
is an infinite Gaussian Hermitian matrix, namely,

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{ii}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\mathcal{N}(0,1),\;\;\forall i\in\mathbb{N},\ \ \Re(\mathbf{G}_{ij}),\;\Im(\mathbf{G}_{ij})\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right),\;\;\forall i<j,

with all independent entries subject to the Hermitian constraint and where
\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sigma^{2}\right)
denotes the centered Gaussian random variable of variance
\sigma^{2}
. Moreover, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}

the
\boldsymbol{\xi(j)}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta(j)}
are infinite vectors of
i.i.d.
(independent, identically distributed)
standard complex Gaussian random variables (real and imaginary parts are independent normally distributed random variables with mean zero and variance
\frac{1}{2}
).

For
\omega\in\Omega_{+}
, we defined, abusing notation,

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\omega}^{\infty}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left(\gamma-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}x_{j}\right)\mathbf{I}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}x_{j}\boldsymbol{\xi}(j)\boldsymbol{\xi}^{{\dagger}}(j),

(42)

where, as before,
\left(\boldsymbol{\xi(j)}\right)_{j=1}^{\infty}
is a sequence of infinite vectors of
i.i.d.
complex Gaussian random variables.

We note that the definition of the matrices
\mathbf{A}_{\omega}^{\infty}
, for
\omega\in\Omega_{+}
, is consistent with viewing
\omega\in\Omega
under the embedding (
39
). We also note that we will never really need to consider the
\mathbf{A}_{\omega}^{\infty}
as infinite matrices but rather only consider them through their
K\times K
top-left corner projections
\Pi_{K}^{\infty}(\mathbf{A}_{\omega}^{\infty})
. We can now define a family of Markov kernels that play a key role in our argument.

Definition 2.18
.

For any
K\in\mathbb{N}
, we define, with (abusing notation) either
\omega\in\Omega
or
\omega\in\Omega_{+}
,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}(\omega,\cdot)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathsf{Law}\left[\mathsf{eval}_{K}\left(\Pi_{K}^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{A}_{\omega}^{\infty}\right)\right)\right].

(43)

The following is easy to see.

Proposition 2.19
.

For any
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
\omega\in\Omega
or
\omega\in\Omega_{+}
,
\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}(\omega,\cdot)
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{W}_{K}
or
\mathbb{W}_{K,+}
respectively. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N}^{\infty}\Lambda_{K}^{N}=\Lambda_{K}^{\infty},\ \ \forall N>K.

Proof.

This first statement is immediate from the definition and the second statement follows by unitary-invariance by virtue of Baryshnikov’s result
[
13
]
.
∎

The kernels
\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}
are Markov and in fact Feller as expected.

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
K\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}f

\displaystyle\in C_{0}(\Omega),\ \ \;\,\forall f\in C_{0}\left(\mathbb{W}_{K}\right),

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}f

\displaystyle\in C_{0}(\Omega_{+}),\ \ \forall f\in C_{0}\left(\mathbb{W}_{K,+}\right).

In particular, the kernels are Feller Markov.

Proof.

Continuity of
\omega\mapsto\Lambda_{N}^{\infty}(\omega,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})
, in the sense of weak convergence, was proven in
[
11
]
(see the proof of Proposition 3.5 therein). From this we also obtain the measurability of
\omega\mapsto\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}(\omega,\mathscr{A})
for measurable
\mathscr{A}
. It remains to prove the vanishing at infinity property. In the case of
\Omega
this was shown in
[
8
]
. We give a more direct argument in the case of
\Omega_{+}
(an adaptation will work for
\Omega
as well). Let
f\in C_{0}\left(\mathbb{W}_{K,+}\right)
be arbitrary.
We need to show that for any sequence
\left(\omega^{(n)}\right)_{n=1}^{\infty}
in
\Omega_{+}
, with
\omega^{(n)}=\left(\mathbf{x}^{(n)},\gamma^{(n)}\right)
, such that
\omega^{(n)}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty
(which means
\gamma^{(n)}\to\infty
), we have
\left(\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}f\right)\left(\omega^{(n)}\right)\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
.
To do so, it suffices to show that when
\gamma^{(n)}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty
, the largest eigenvalue of
\Pi_{K}^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{A}_{\omega^{(n)}}^{\infty}\right)
converges to infinity in distribution. Since the eigenvalues of sub-matrices of different orders interlace (Cauchy interlacing theorem), we only need to prove the
K=1
case. Note that, from the explicit formula (
42
), the corresponding random variable
\mathsf{x}_{n}
is simply

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\gamma^{(n)}-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}x_{j}^{(n)}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{x_{j}^{(n)}},

where each
\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\theta}
denotes a gamma random variable with Laplace transform
z\mapsto(1+\theta z)^{-1}
and they are all independent. Thus, we want to show that

\mathsf{x}_{n}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty

when
\gamma^{(n)}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty
. We have two possibilities; either
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}x_{j}^{(n)}
remains bounded, in which case the result follows immediately, otherwise,
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}x_{j}^{(n)}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty
which implies,
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{x_{j}^{(n)}}{\longrightarrow}\infty
in distribution. This last claim can easily be seen by virtue of the fact that, for any
z,R\geq 0
, we have,

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{x_{j}^{(n)}}\leq R\right)\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-z\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{x_{j}^{(n)}}\right)\right]\mathrm{e}^{zR}\leq\frac{\mathrm{e}^{zR}}{1+z\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}x_{j}^{(n)}}.

This completes the proof.
∎

The following fundamental result is due to Pickrell
[
89
]
and Olshanski and Vershik
[
81
]
, in equivalent form (modulo the Feller property of the kernels). In rather less obvious equivalent form, it can be traced back to the work of Schoenberg
[
3
,
53
]
on totally positive functions, see
[
81
]
. It was translated to the setting of projective systems in
[
8
]
. A proof, different from
[
89
,
81
]
, directly in this setup and a generalisation is given in
[
11
]
.

Theorem 2.21
.

The space
\Omega
is a Feller boundary of
\left(\mathbb{W}_{N},\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
.

The following result is again due to Olshanski and Vershik in equivalent form (modulo the Feller property of the kernels), see Remark 2.11 in
[
81
]
, .

Theorem 2.22
.

The space
\Omega_{+}
is a Feller boundary of
\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+},\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
.

The uniform approximation theorem that we state below, could also be used to prove the results above, by following the argument in Section 3 of
[
18
]
, modulo an independent proof (we cannot use Lemma
2.4
as the argument would be circular) of the density of
\cup_{N}\Lambda_{N}^{\infty}C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N})
and
\cup_{N}\Lambda_{N}^{\infty}C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
in
C_{0}(\Omega)
and
C_{0}(\Omega_{+})
respectively. In the combinatorial setting of the Gelfand-Tsetlin graph this is not too difficult, see Section 2.7 in
[
18
]
, but in the continuous setting there are additional subtleties. We will not pursue this here.

2.4

Uniform Approximation theorem

Let
(\mathcal{E}_{N})_{N=1}^{\infty}=\left(\mathbb{W}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
or
\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
. Let
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
denote the space of (real-valued) smooth and compactly supported functions on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
. It will be very convenient in what follows to consider the symmetric extension of the kernels given in (
34
) and (
43
) to
\mathbb{R}^{K}
, which we denote by the same notation. Thus, both
\Lambda_{K}^{N}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})
and
\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}(\omega,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})
can be viewed as positive measures on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
with total mass
K!
(we choose not to normalise them to be probability measures as it will be more convenient) and can act on functions defined on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
(this will be clear from context). The following is the main technical result of this part of the paper.

Theorem 2.23
.

Let
K\in\mathbb{N}
.
For all
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
,
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}}\left|\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|\to 0,\ \ \textnormal{as }N\to\infty.

(44)

The proof of this theorem will be given in Subsection
2.6
. The following function spaces will appear frequently below.

Definition 2.24
.

Define
C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
to be the space of smooth compactly supported functions on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
that are moreover symmetric in their variables. Then, we define

C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathcal{E}_{K})
to be the space of functions on
\mathcal{E}_{K}
which are restrictions of functions
C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
to
\mathcal{E}_{K}
.

Observe that, by definition

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{E}_{K}}g(\mathbf{y})\Lambda_{K}^{N}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}g(\mathbf{y})\Lambda_{K}^{N}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{E}_{K}}g(\mathbf{y})\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}(\omega,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}g(\mathbf{y})\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}(\omega,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}),\ \ \forall g\in C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K}).

We have the following corollary of Theorem
2.23
.

Corollary 2.25
.

Let
K\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
g\in C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathcal{E}_{K})
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}}\left|\int_{\mathcal{E}_{K}}g\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right),\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\right)-\int_{\mathcal{E}_{K}}g(\mathbf{y})\Lambda_{K}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\right)\right|\to 0,\ \ \textnormal{as }N\to\infty.

(45)

2.5

Approximation of Markov processes

In this subsection we show how using the uniform approximation theorem above and an underlying intertwining we can prove a path-space convergence result to the abstract Feller process associated to
\left(\mathsf{P}_{\infty}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
.

To be precise, the running assumption is the following. In the setting of Subsection
2.1
, let
(\mathcal{E}_{N})_{N=1}^{\infty}=\left(\mathbb{W}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
or
\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
and

\left(\mathcal{L}_{N}^{N+1}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}=\left(\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
. Namely, we consider the systems
\left(\mathbb{W}_{N},\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
and
\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+},\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
,
with
\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}
given by (
34
). As we have seen in Theorems
2.21
and
2.22
, the Feller boundaries of these systems are given by
\Omega
and
\Omega_{+}
respectively.

We moreover consider, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, a Feller Markov process
\left(\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
, with continuous sample paths,
on
\mathcal{E}_{N}
with transition semigroup
\left(\mathsf{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
and we assume that these semigroups are intertwined:

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{N+1}(t)\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}=\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\mathsf{P}_{N}(t),\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\;\forall N\in\mathbb{N}.

(46)

Let
\left(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\Omega};t\geq 0\right)
and

\left(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\Omega_{+}};t\geq 0\right)

be the corresponding Feller processes on
\Omega
and
\Omega_{+}
constructed as in Theorem
2.6
, respectively. We denote the Feller semigroup of the process on the boundary, abusing notation, for either
\Omega
or
\Omega_{+}
, by
(\mathsf{P}_{\infty}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.
Moreover, recall that we have

\mathsf{P}_{\infty}(t)\Lambda_{N}^{\infty}=\Lambda_{N}^{\infty}\mathsf{P}_{N}(t),\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\;\forall N\in\mathbb{N}.

(47)

We need two abstract definitions that we will specialise to our setting shortly.

Definition 2.26
.

Suppose
\left(\mathscr{L}_{n}\right)_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a sequence of Banach spaces, with the norm of
\mathscr{L}_{n}
denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{n}
. Let also
\mathscr{L}
be another Banach space with norm denoted by
\|\cdot\|
.
We say that a sequence of vectors
(\mathbf{f}_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
with
\mathbf{f}_{n}\in\mathscr{L}_{n}
approximates a vector
\mathbf{f}\in\mathscr{L}
, and we write
\mathbf{f}_{n}\to\mathbf{f}
, if and only if there exist bounded linear operators
\pi_{n}:\mathscr{L}\to\mathscr{L}_{n}
, satisfying
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\||\pi_{n}\||_{n}<\infty
, where
\||\cdot\||_{n}
denotes the operator norm of linear operators from
\mathscr{L}
to
\mathscr{L}_{n}
, such that,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\big\|\mathbf{f}_{n}-\pi_{n}\mathbf{f}\big\|_{n}=0.

Definition 2.27
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{N}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}
be the generators (defined in an analogous way to Definition
2.7
) corresponding to strongly continuous semigroups of contractions on the Banach spaces
\mathscr{L}_{N}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\infty}
, respectively. We say that
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}
is approximated by
\left(\mathcal{A}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}

if for any
\mathbf{f}
in a core (defined in an analogous way to Definition
2.8
) of
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}
, there exist a sequence
\mathbf{f}_{N}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{N})
such that
\mathbf{f}_{N}\to\mathbf{f}
and
\mathcal{A}_{N}\mathbf{f}_{N}\to\mathcal{A}_{\infty}\mathbf{f}
.

The following proposition is key for our purposes. Here, we take the Banach spaces to be
\mathscr{L}_{N}=C_{0}(\mathcal{E}_{N})
and
\mathscr{L}=C_{0}(\mathcal{E}_{\infty})
and the implicit maps
\pi_{N}:C_{0}(\mathcal{E}_{\infty})\to C_{0}(\mathcal{E}_{N})
to be the ones induced by embeddings of
\mathcal{E}_{N}
into
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
, namely (
40
) or (
41
).

Proposition 2.28
.

In the setting described above, we let
\mathsf{L}_{N}
and
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}
denote the generators of the Feller semigroups
\left(\mathsf{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
and
\left(\mathsf{P}_{\infty}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
, respectively. Let
\mathscr{C}_{N}=C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathcal{E}_{N})
and
\mathscr{C}_{\infty}
be as in (
30
). Moreover, assume that, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathscr{C}_{N}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{L}_{N})
and
\mathsf{L}_{N}\mathscr{C}_{N}\subset\mathscr{C}_{N}
. Then, for any
\mathbf{f}\in\mathscr{C}_{\infty}
, there exists a sequence
(\mathbf{f}_{N})_{N=1}^{\infty}
with
\mathbf{f}_{N}\in\mathscr{C}_{N}

such that
\mathbf{f}_{N}\to\mathbf{f}
and
\mathsf{L}_{N}\mathbf{f}_{N}\to\mathsf{L}_{\infty}\mathbf{f}
.

An immediate consequence of Proposition
2.28
and Lemma
2.9
is the following result.

Corollary 2.29
.

In the setting of Proposition
2.28
, suppose moreover, that for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathscr{C}_{N}

is a core for
\mathsf{L}_{N}
. Then,
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}
is approximated by
\mathsf{L}_{N}
in the sense of Definition
2.27
.

The two main ingredients to prove Proposition
2.28
is
Theorem
2.23
, in the form of Corollary
2.25
, and the intertwining relation (
47
). Before giving the proof we obtain as an immediate consequence the convergence of the corresponding Markov dynamics on path space.

Theorem 2.30
.

In the setting described above, let
\mathcal{E}_{N}=\mathbb{W}_{N}
and

\left(\mathbf{X}^{(N)}_{t};t\geq 0\right)
be the process on
\Omega
corresponding to the Feller process
\left(\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
, having continuous trajectories, with transition semigroup
\left(\mathsf{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
,
under the embedding (
40
) of
\mathbb{W}_{N}
into
\Omega
. Suppose moreover that, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathscr{C}_{N}=C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N})

is a core, and invariant under
\mathsf{L}_{N}
, the generator of
\left(\mathsf{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
. Then, if
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}_{0}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}^{\Omega}_{0}
, we have that,

\mathbf{X}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}^{\Omega},\ \ \textnormal{in }C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\Omega).

Proof.

Using Corollary
2.29
(recall that the implicit maps
\pi_{N}
therein are induced by the embeddings (
40
) of
\mathbb{W}_{N}
into
\Omega
), it follows from
[
34
]
[Chapter 4, Theorem 2.11] that
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}\longrightarrow\mathbf{X}^{\Omega}
, in distribution, in
D(\mathbb{R}_{+},\Omega)

the space of càdlàg functions on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with values in
\Omega
endowed with the Skorokhod topology, see
[
34
]
.
The desired result is then concluded from the well-known fact that the space
C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\Omega)
is closed in
D(\mathbb{R}_{+},\Omega)
in the Skorokhod topology and recalling that, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\Omega)
.
∎

An analogous statement holds when the processes live in
\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
. The proof is the same.

Theorem 2.31
.

In the setting described above, let
\mathcal{E}_{N}=\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
and

\left(\mathbf{X}^{(N)}_{t};t\geq 0\right)
be the process on
\Omega_{+}
corresponding to the Feller process
\left(\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
, having continuous trajectories, with semigroup
\left(\mathsf{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}

under the embedding (
41
) of
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
into
\Omega_{+}
. Suppose moreover that, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathscr{C}_{N}=C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})

is a core, and invariant under
\mathsf{L}_{N}
, the generator of
\left(\mathsf{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
. Then, if
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}_{0}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}_{0}
, we have that,

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}},\ \ \textnormal{in }C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\Omega_{+}).

(48)

Let us write
\mathbf{X}_{\cdot}^{\Omega_{+}}=\left((\mathsf{x}_{i}(\cdot))_{i=1}^{\infty},\boldsymbol{\gamma}(\cdot)\right)
below for the process on
\Omega_{+}
in coordinates and similarly
\mathbf{X}_{\cdot}^{(N)}=\left((\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(\cdot))_{i=1}^{\infty},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(N)}(\cdot)\right)
for the embedded process (recall
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(N)}=\sum\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}
).

Remark 2.32
.

Observe that, Theorem
2.31
, in particular implies that

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{x}_{i},\ \ \textnormal{in }C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+}),\ \ \forall i\in\mathbb{N},\ \textnormal{ and }\ \sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\gamma},\ \ \textnormal{in }C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+}).

(49)

Proposition 2.33
.

In the setting of Theorem
2.31
, there exists a coupling of the
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}
and
\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}
on a single probability space such that, almost surely, for any
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

(50)

Proof.

We use the Skorokhod representation theorem to couple
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}
and
\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}
on a single probability space, and, abusing notation, denote the new processes by the same symbol. One can then write for all
M\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\,2\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=M+1}^{\infty}\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)\right)^{2}+2\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=M+1}^{\infty}\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be given. Observe first that as
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}<\infty
, there exists
M_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for all
M\geq M_{0}
,

\displaystyle 2\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=M+1}^{\infty}\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}.

Then, using Theorem
2.31
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=1}^{M_{0}}\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{i}(t)\right)^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4},

for large enough
N
.
Note moreover that,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=M_{0}+1}^{\infty}\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)\right)^{2}\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathsf{x}_{M_{0}}^{(N)}(t)\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=M_{0}}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t).

Now, from (
49
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathsf{x}_{M_{0}}^{(N)}(t)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathsf{x}_{M_{0}}(t),\ \ \ \sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=M_{0}}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}c<\infty.

Hence, one can pick a possibly larger
M_{0}
such that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathsf{x}_{M_{0}}(t)<\frac{\varepsilon}{4c}
also holds, and thus,

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\geq 1}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{i=M_{0}+1}^{\infty}\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)\right)^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}.

Putting everything together, we conclude (
50
).
∎

When considering processes on
\mathbb{W}_{N}
and
\Omega
instead, a very similar argument can be used to show the following convergence in
\ell^{3}
as a consequence of Theorem
2.30
. Below we write
\mathbf{X}_{\cdot}^{\Omega}=\left(\left(\mathsf{x}^{+}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty},\left(\mathsf{x}^{-}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty},\boldsymbol{\gamma}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\delta}(\cdot)\right)
for the process on
\Omega
in coordinates and similarly
\mathbf{X}_{\cdot}^{(N)}=\left(\left(\mathsf{x}^{(N),+}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty},\left(\mathsf{x}^{(N),-}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(N)}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\delta}^{(N)}(\cdot)\right)
for the embedded process.

Proposition 2.34
.

In the setting of Theorem
2.30
, there exists a coupling of the
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}
and
\mathbf{X}^{\Omega}
on a single probability space such that, almost surely, for any
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N),+}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{i}^{+}(t)\right|^{3}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N),-}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-}(t)\right|^{3}\right)\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

(51)

We now prove Proposition
2.28
.

Proof of Proposition
2.28
.

We prove the proposition in the case
\left(\mathcal{E}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}=\left(\mathbb{W}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
. The exact same argument works for
\left(\mathcal{E}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}=\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
.

We then take the Banach spaces
\left(\mathscr{L}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}=\left(C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N})\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
and
\mathscr{L}=C_{0}(\Omega)
in the Definition
2.26
.
Considering the definition of
\mathscr{C}_{\infty}
from Lemma
2.9
, it only suffices to prove the statement for functions of the form
\mathbf{f}=\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}g
with some
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
g\in\mathscr{C}_{K}
.
We thus fix a positive integer
K
, and take an arbitrary function
g\in\mathscr{C}_{K}
. For any
N>K
, let

\mathbf{f}_{N}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\Lambda_{K}^{N}g
and
\mathbf{f}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}g
. Observe that, by the Feller property of the kernels,

\mathbf{f}_{N}\in C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N})
and
\mathbf{f}\in C_{0}(\Omega)
, respectively. We want to show that

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{N}\to\mathbf{f},\ \ \text{ as }N\to\infty,

(52)

in the sense of Definition
2.26
. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we then define the operator
\pi_{N}:C_{0}(\Omega)\to C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N})
as follows

\displaystyle(\pi_{N}f)\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)=f\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right),\ \ \mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},

(53)

where by
\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)
we mean the embedding given in (
40
). It is easy to see that the operator norm
\||\pi_{N}\||_{N}\leq 1
, uniformly in
N
, and so the conditions given in Definition
2.26
are satisfied. We thus need to check that

\displaystyle\big\|\pi_{N}\mathbf{f}-\mathbf{f}_{N}\big\|_{N}=\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|(\pi_{N}\mathbf{f})\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)-\mathbf{f}_{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

Note that this is exactly the expression in (
45
) from Corollary
2.25
, and so (
52
) is verified. We next want to show that

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{N}\mathbf{f}_{N}\to\mathsf{L}_{\infty}\mathbf{f},\ \ \text{ as }N\to\infty,

also holds, that is,

\displaystyle\big\|\pi_{N}(\mathsf{L}_{\infty}\mathbf{f})-\mathsf{L}_{N}\mathbf{f}_{N}\big\|_{N}\to 0,\ \ \text{ as }N\to\infty.

(54)

First, it follows from the intertwining relations (
46
) and (
47
) between the semigroups that for all
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{L}_{K})
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{N}\Lambda_{K}^{N}f

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{K}^{N}\mathsf{L}_{K}f,\ \ \forall N>K,

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{\infty}\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}f

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}\mathsf{L}_{K}f.

In particular, one can check that

\mathbf{f}_{N}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{L}_{N})
. We can then write

\displaystyle\big\|\pi_{N}(\mathsf{L}_{\infty}\mathbf{f})-\mathsf{L}_{N}\mathbf{f}_{N}\big\|_{N}=\big\|\pi_{N}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\infty}\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}g\right)-\mathsf{L}_{N}\Lambda_{K}^{N}g\big\|_{N}=\big\|\pi_{N}\left(\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}\mathsf{L}_{K}g\right)-\Lambda_{K}^{N}\mathsf{L}_{K}g\big\|_{N}.

Note moreover that, by assumption, the space
\mathscr{C}_{K}
is invariant under the action of
\mathsf{L}_{K}
and thus
\mathsf{L}_{K}g\in\mathscr{C}_{K}
. Hence, (
54
) follows exactly in the same way as (
52
) from Corollary
2.25
.
∎

2.6

Proof of the uniform approximation theorem

We only consider the case
\left(\mathcal{E}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}=\left(\mathbb{W}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
throughout this section. It is not hard to see, by inspecting (
44
) and the relevant definitions carefully, that in the case
\left(\mathcal{E}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}=\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
we are simply taking the supremum over
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
instead of
\mathbb{W}_{N}
while the quantity we are taking the supremum of is exactly the same as for
\mathbb{W}_{N}
. Thus, the result for
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
is a special case of the one over
\mathbb{W}_{N}
. We need some preliminaries.

2.6.1
Explicit formulae of Markov kernels

We first present an explicit formula for
\Lambda_{K}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\right)
which is the starting point in our analysis. For any
N\geq 2
and
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}^{\circ}
, we consider the fundamental spline function
\mathcal{M}\left(y;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, with knots
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}
, given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\left(y;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)=(N-1)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\left(x_{i}^{(N)}-y\right)_{+}^{N-2}}{\prod_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\left(x_{i}^{(N)}-x_{j}^{(N)}\right)},

(55)

where, for
z\in\mathbb{R}
,
(z)_{+}=\max\{z,0\}
. One can check that, for all
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}^{\circ}
,
y\mapsto\mathcal{M}\left(y;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)
is a piecewise polynomial function with
N-2
continuous derivatives. Moreover, it satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{M}\left(y;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\mathrm{d}y=1.

The next theorem, proven by Olshanski in
[
77
]
, gives an expression for the Markov kernel
\Lambda_{K}^{N}
defined in (
35
) in terms of the spline function introduced above.

Theorem 2.35
.

Let
N\geq 2
and
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}^{\circ}
. Then, for any
1\leq K\leq N-1
, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{l=1}^{K-1}\binom{N-K+l}{l}\frac{\det{\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{M}\left(y_{K-j+1};x_{N-i+1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{K-i+1}^{(N)}\right)\end{pmatrix}}_{i,j=1}^{K}}{\prod_{j-i\geq N-K+1}\left(x_{i}^{(N)}-x_{j}^{(N)}\right)}\Delta_{K}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y},\ \ \forall\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{K}.

(56)

We note that, the case
K=1
, boils down to the following identity

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)},\mathrm{d}y\right)=\mathcal{M}\left(y;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\mathrm{d}y,\ \ \forall\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}^{\circ},

(57)

which is an observation first due to Okounkov
[
81
]
.

The formula from Theorem
2.35
is not yet amenable to asymptotic analysis, partly due to the denominator, which may vanish and lead to singularities. Thankfully, we can use a trick based on a formula for derivatives of spline functions to get rid of the denominator entirely. The new formula for
\Lambda_{K}^{N}
will however involve derivatives of the spline function
\mathcal{M}
which when we extend in the sequel the formula to
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}
with coinciding coordinates will need to be interpreted in a weak sense.

Theorem 2.36
.

In the setting of Theorem
2.35
, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\right)=\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K-1}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\det\left(\mathcal{M}^{(K-i)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K-j+1};x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N-i+1}^{(N)}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{K}\Delta_{K}\left(\mathbf{y}\right).

(58)

Here, for any
j=1,\dots,K-1
,
\mathcal{M}^{(j)}
denotes the
j
-th derivative of the function
\mathcal{M}
with the convention
\mathcal{M}^{(0)}=\mathcal{M}
,
and,
\mathcal{M}^{(j)}(\mathrm{d}y,\cdot)=\mathcal{M}^{(j)}(y,\cdot)\mathrm{d}y

Proof.

We have following relation for the derivative of the spline function in (
55
), obtained by direct computation,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}\left(y;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)=\frac{N-1}{x_{1}^{(N)}-x_{N}^{(N)}}\left(\mathcal{M}\left(y;x_{2}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N}^{(N)}\right)-\mathcal{M}\left(y;x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N-1}^{(N)}\right)\right).

(59)

We now consider the formula given in Theorem (
2.35
) and repeatedly apply elementary row operations to the determinant in (
56
) and use (
59
) to obtain (
58
).
∎

Let

g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})

and consider

\Lambda_{K}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)
for
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}^{\circ}
. We have
from (
58
) that

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\Lambda_{K}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\right)g\left(\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K-1}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\det\left(\mathcal{M}^{(K-i)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K-j+1};x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N-i+1}^{(N)}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{K}\Delta_{K}(\mathbf{y})g(\mathbf{y}).

Next, one can expand the determinant and use integration by parts for each term in the expansion to rewrite the above expression as

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\frac{K(K-1)}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}\textnormal{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{j=1}^{K}\Lambda_{1}^{N-K+j}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{\sigma(j)};x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N-K+j}^{(N)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt\times\partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\sigma}\left(\Delta_{K}(\mathbf{y})g(\mathbf{y})\right),

(60)

where
we used (
57
) and for
\sigma
in
\mathbb{S}_{K}
, the symmetric group of degree
K
, the differential operator
\partial^{\sigma}
acts on functions
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
as follows:

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\sigma}h(\mathbf{y})=\frac{\partial^{\frac{K(K-1)}{2}}}{\partial y_{1}^{\sigma(1)-1}\partial y_{2}^{\sigma(2)-1}\cdots\partial y_{K}^{\sigma(K)-1}}h(\mathbf{y}).

(61)

Now suppose
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
is arbitrary. Take a sequence
\left(\mathbf{x}_{n}\right)_{n=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathbb{W}_{N}^{\circ}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{n}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{x}
. It then follows from Lemma
2.14
that both the left and right hand side of equality (
60
) above, when evaluating at
\mathbf{x}_{n}
, converge to the corresponding terms for
\mathbf{x}
, as
n\to\infty
. This gives an explicit expression of
\Lambda_{K}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}\right)
for all
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
and general
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
.
Note that, this expression matches the one we obtain by formally expanding the following determinant

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K-1}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\det\left(\mathcal{M}^{(K-i)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K-j+1};x_{1},\dots,x_{N-i+1}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{K}\Delta_{K}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)g\left(\mathbf{y}\right),

where, the derivatives correspond to the distributional derivative
of the kernel
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{d}y,\cdot)
. In particular, it is consistent with the formal expansion of the determinant along rows and columns. From now on, all appearances of these distributional determinants will be understood to be interpreted in the weak sense explained above when applied to
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
functions (and the functions we need to apply them to will always be in this space).

We next present an analogous explicit formula for the kernel
\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}
. We do this in the case of
\Omega
. The case of
\Omega_{+}
is in fact a special case under the embedding (
39
). So, suppose
\omega\in\Omega
is arbitrary and written in coordinates as
\omega=\left(\mathbf{x}^{+},\mathbf{x}^{-},\gamma,\delta\right)
. We will make very frequent use of the following notation in everything that follows.

Definition 2.37
.

For
\omega\in\Omega
, or, abusing notation, for
\omega\in\Omega_{+}
, we define the probability measure
\phi_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}y)
on
\mathbb{R}
by,

\phi_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}y)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\Lambda_{1}^{\infty}(\omega,\mathrm{d}y).

(62)

Given arbitrary
\omega\in\Omega
as above, we define, for
\epsilon>0
,
\omega_{\epsilon}\in\Omega
by:

\omega_{\epsilon}=\left(\mathbf{x}^{+},\mathbf{x}^{-},\gamma,\delta+\epsilon\right).

We observe that, since
\phi_{\omega_{\epsilon}}
involves a Gaussian component, it has a smooth density with respect to Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}
, see
[
81
]
, which we denote by, abusing notation,
\phi_{\omega_{\epsilon}}(y)
. We write
\phi_{\omega_{\epsilon}}^{(\ell)}(\mathrm{d}y)=\phi_{\omega_{\epsilon}}^{(\ell)}(y)\mathrm{d}y
for its
\ell
-th derivative. We have the following result.

Proposition 2.38
.

Let
\epsilon>0
. Then, the Markov kernel
\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}(\omega_{\epsilon},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})
has an explicit density with respect to Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{W}_{K}
and is given by,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}\left(\omega_{\epsilon},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\right)=\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K-1}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\det\left(\phi_{\omega_{\epsilon}}^{(j-1)}(\mathrm{d}y_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{K}\Delta_{K}(\mathbf{y})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}.

(63)

Proof.

This is implicit in the proof of Theorem 7.7 in
[
81
]
.
∎

Recall (or simply observe) that, as
\epsilon\to 0
, we have the weak convergence

\Lambda^{\infty}_{K}(\omega_{\epsilon},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\longrightarrow\Lambda^{\infty}_{K}(\omega,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}).

(64)

Now, given
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
, we expand the determinant in (
63
) and use integration by parts as before and then take
\epsilon\to 0
by virtue of (
64
) to make sense of the potentially distributional determinant, for general
\omega\in\Omega
,

\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K-1}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\det\left(\phi_{\omega}^{(j-1)}(\mathrm{d}y_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{K}\Delta_{K}(\mathbf{y}),

as follows, when acting on
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}\left(\omega,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\right)g(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\frac{K(K-1)}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\prod_{j=1}^{K}\phi_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}y_{j})\sum_{\sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}\textnormal{sgn}(\sigma)\partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\sigma}\left(\Delta_{K}(\mathbf{y})g(\mathbf{y})\right),

where the differential operator is the same as in (
61
).

As before, this weak-sense definition is consistent with the formal expansion of the determinant along rows and columns. From now on, all appearances of these distributional determinants will be understood to be interpreted in the weak sense explained above when applied to
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
functions (all functions we need to apply them to will always be in this space).

2.6.2
Proof of Theorem
2.23

For any fixed
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
N>K
, and
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
, we consider the following variant of
\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right),\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K-1}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\det\left(\phi_{\omega\left(x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N-K+j}^{(N)}\right)}^{(j-1)}(\mathrm{d}y_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{K}\Delta_{K}(\mathbf{y}).

(65)

We note that we only need (and in fact only define)
\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}
for
\omega\in\Omega
of the form
\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)
for
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
. Again the determinant in equation (
65
) above is interpreted in the weak sense when tested against smooth functions of compact support as explained previously. We first prove the desired result with

\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}
replaced by

\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}
in (
44
).

Theorem 2.39
.

In the setting of Theorem
2.23
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|\to 0,\ \ \textnormal{as }N\to\infty.

After establishing Theorem
2.39
, we prove that
\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)
and
\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)
are asymptotically uniformly close as
N\to\infty
, see Proposition
2.43
for the exact statement, which concludes the desired result.
The proof of Theorem
2.39
is by an inductive argument in
K
. The variant
\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}
of
\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}
is very well-adapted to proving the rather complicated inductive step and this is its raison d’être. Before giving the proof, we need some more preliminaries and notations.

Denote the Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms of an integrable function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
\hat{f}
and
\check{f}
respectively given by:

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}\xi_{j}}f(\mathbf{y})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y},\ \ \check{f}(\boldsymbol{\xi})=(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathrm{e}^{-\textnormal{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}\xi_{j}}f(\mathbf{y})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y},\ \ \boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Definition 2.40
.

We define the following functions

\displaystyle F_{+}(z;\omega)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}\gamma z}\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\textnormal{i}x_{j}z}}{1-\textnormal{i}x_{j}z},\ \ \forall z\in\mathbb{R},\ \ \omega\in\Omega_{+},

(66)

\displaystyle F(z;\omega)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}\gamma z-\frac{\delta-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left[(x_{i}^{+})^{2}+(x_{i}^{-})^{2}\right]}{2}z^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\textnormal{i}x_{j}^{+}z}}{1-\textnormal{i}x_{j}^{+}z}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}x_{j}^{-}z}}{1+\textnormal{i}x_{j}^{-}z},\ \ \forall z\in\mathbb{R},\ \ \omega\in\Omega.

(67)

Note that, the infinite products are well-defined by virtue of
\omega\in\Omega_{+}
and
\omega\in\Omega
respectively.

It is easy to check that
F_{+}(\cdot;\omega)
and
F(\cdot;\omega)
are
the characteristic functions (i.e. Fourier transforms) of the probability law
\phi_{\omega}
when
\omega\in\Omega_{+}
and
\Omega
, respectively. In particular, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}F(z;\omega)g(z)\mathrm{d}z=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{\omega}(\mathrm{d}z)\hat{g}(z),\ \ \forall g\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}\right),

(68)

where
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
denotes the space of Schwartz functions on
\mathbb{R}
. We will also need the following formula in the argument.

Lemma 2.41
.

Let
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zx_{j}^{(N)}}{N}\right)^{-1}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zy}{N}\right)^{-N}\mathcal{M}\left(\mathrm{d}y;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right).

(69)

Proof.

See Section 8 of
[
81
]
, stated there for
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}^{\circ}
which can be extended to coinciding coordinates by continuity.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.39
(the base case).

Let
K=1
and fix
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Observe that
\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\infty}=\Lambda_{1}^{\infty}
.
We have by definition that

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{1}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}(\mathrm{d}y)g(y)-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}\left(\mathrm{d}y;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)g(y)\right|.

We have, again by definition,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}(\mathrm{d}y)g(y)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}F\left(z;\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)(z)\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z.

Using (
67
) and then (
69
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}(\mathrm{d}y)g(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zx_{j}^{(N)}}{N}\right)^{-1}\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zw}{N}\right)^{-N}\mathcal{M}\left(\mathrm{d}w;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z.

Note now that for any fix
w\in\mathbb{R}
,
\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zw}{N}\right)^{-N}
converges pointwise to
\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}wz}
. Also, since

g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
the function
\check{g}
is integrable on
\mathbb{R}
. Thus, by dominated convergence theorem,

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{N}(w)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zw}{N}\right)^{-N}\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z-g(w)

(70)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zw}{N}\right)^{-N}-\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}zw}\right]\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z\,\rightarrow 0,\ \ \textnormal{as }\;N\rightarrow\infty.

We show in the Lemma
2.42
below that the above convergence is uniform over
\mathbb{R}
which proves the result for the case
K=1
. This is because by the uniform convergence, we have for
N
large enough that
\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathsf{G}_{N}(w)|<\varepsilon
, for a given
\varepsilon>0
,
and accordingly,

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}(\mathrm{d}y)g(y)-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}\left(\mathrm{d}y;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)g(y)\right|=\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathsf{G}_{N}(w)\mathcal{M}\left(\mathrm{d}w;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\ \ =\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathsf{G}_{N}(w)\right|\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}\left(\mathrm{d}w;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|<\varepsilon.

Here, we used the fact that
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}\left(\mathrm{d}w;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)=1
for every
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
.
∎

Lemma 2.42
.

Let
\mathsf{G}_{N}
be the function defined in (
70
). Then, we have,

\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathsf{G}_{N}(w)|\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that the assertion is not true. Thus, there exist
\delta>0
and a sequence
(w_{N_{n}})_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{G}_{N_{n}}(w_{N_{n}})\right|>\delta,\ \ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

(71)

There are two possibilities:

(i)

There exists some positive constant
L

such that
|w_{N_{n}}|\leq L
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii)

The sequence
(w_{N_{n}})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is unbounded.

We show both of these cases lead to a contradiction.
First we assume (i) occurs. Consider the sequence of functions

(\mathsf{G}_{N_{n}})_{n=1}^{\infty}

on the interval
[-L,L]
. One can see that this is a sequence of uniformly bounded and
equicontinuous functions
on
[-L,L]
, and hence by the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem, there exists a subsequence
\left(\mathsf{G}_{N_{n_{k}}}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converging uniformly to 0 on
[-L,L]
. This implies that for large enough
k
we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{G}_{N_{n_{k}}}(w)\right|<\delta,\ \ \forall w\in[-L,L],

which contradicts (
71
).

Next, we consider (ii), namely
|w_{N_{n}}|\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
Then, there exists a non-decreasing, in absolute value, subsequence of
(w_{N_{n}})_{n=1}^{\infty}
diverging to infinity. We denote this subsequence using the same index, and thus, we have

|w_{N_{1}}|\leq|w_{N_{2}}|\leq\dots\longrightarrow\infty
.
Suppose first that

\lim_{N_{n}\to\infty}\frac{|w_{N_{n}}|}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}=0
,
and let

\displaystyle f_{n}(z)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}}{N_{n}}\right)^{-N_{n}}-\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}},\ \ z\in\mathbb{R}.

One can easily check that

f_{n}
converges to
0
pointwise,
and so does
\mathsf{G}_{N_{n}}(w_{N_{n}})
. This again violates (
71
).
Finally, we assume that

\lim_{N_{n}\to\infty}\frac{|w_{N_{n}}|}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}>0
, namely it is equal to a positive constant
\ell
, or
\infty
.
Note that in this case,
g(w_{N_{n}})=0
for large enough
n
.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}}{N_{n}}\right)^{-N_{n}}\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z,\ \ n\in\mathbb{N},

We show in the following that
\mathcal{I}_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
in both cases.
As a result, we conclude in view of (
70
), that
|\mathsf{G}_{N_{n}}(w_{N_{n}})|<\delta
for sufficiently large
n
, which contradicts (
71
) as well, and completes the proof of the lemma.

Assume first that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{|w_{N_{n}}|}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}=\infty
.
Observe that in this case,

\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}}{N_{n}}\right)^{-N_{n}}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
,
and hence,
\mathcal{I}_{n}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
(with the same reasoning as before) as desired. Next, suppose
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{|w_{N_{n}}|}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}=\ell>0
. Since
\check{g}
is integrable, one can choose
M_{\delta}>0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-M_{\delta},M_{\delta})}\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z\leq\frac{\delta}{8}.

Note also that
\left|\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}}{N_{n}}\right)^{-N_{n}}\right|\leq 1
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. So
we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{n}|\leq\left|\int_{-M_{\delta}}^{M_{\delta}}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}}{N_{n}}\right)^{-N_{n}}\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z\right|+\frac{\delta}{8}.

Now, since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{|w_{N_{n}}|}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}=\ell
, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\frac{|w_{N_{n}}|}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\leq\ell+1
for all
n\geq n_{0}
. We pick such an
n_{0}
so that
N_{n_{0}}>M_{\delta}^{2}(\ell+1)^{2}
also holds, on account of which we can write a Taylor expansion for the function
\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}}{N_{n}}\right)^{-N_{n}}
on the interval
(-M_{\delta},M_{\delta})
.
Accordingly, we have for all
z\in(-M_{\delta},M_{\delta})
and
n\geq n_{0}
,

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}}{N_{n}}\right)^{-N_{n}}=\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}-\frac{z^{2}w_{N_{n}}^{2}}{N_{n}}+R_{n}(z)},

where
R_{n}(z)=\frac{z^{3}w_{N_{n}}^{3}}{3N_{n}^{2}}+\frac{z^{4}w_{N_{n}}^{4}}{4N_{n}^{3}}+\dots=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{n}}}\right)
.
Then,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{-M_{\delta}}^{M_{\delta}}\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}-\frac{z^{2}w_{N_{n}}^{2}}{N_{n}}+R_{n}(z)}\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z\right|+\frac{\delta}{8}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{-M_{\delta}}^{M_{\delta}}\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}-\frac{z^{2}w_{N_{n}}^{2}}{N_{n}}}\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z\right|+\left|\int_{-M_{\delta}}^{M_{\delta}}\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}-\frac{z^{2}w_{N_{n}}^{2}}{N_{n}}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{R_{n}(z)}-1\right)\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z\right|+\frac{\delta}{8}.

The second integral in the above expression converges to
0
as
n\to\infty
, since
\left(\mathrm{e}^{R_{n}(z)}-1\right)\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
and the integrand is bounded by
\check{g}
up to a multiplicative constant on
[-M_{\delta},M_{\delta}]
. Thus, for large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we get

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{-M_{\delta}}^{M_{\delta}}\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}-\frac{z^{2}w_{N_{n}}^{2}}{N_{n}}}\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z\right|+\frac{2\delta}{8}\leq\left|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}-\frac{z^{2}w_{N_{n}}^{2}}{N_{n}}}\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z\right|+\frac{3\delta}{8}.

Note now that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the function

\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{z^{2}w_{N_{n}}^{2}}{N_{n}}}\check{g}(z)

is a Schwartz function and the Fourier transform maps the Schwartz space to itself.
It thus follows that for
n
large enough, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\textnormal{i}zw_{N_{n}}-\frac{z^{2}w_{N_{n}}^{2}}{N_{n}}}\check{g}(z)\mathrm{d}z\right|<\frac{\delta}{8},

and hence,

\left|\mathcal{I}_{n}\right|<\frac{\delta}{2}
.
This finally concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.39
(the inductive step).

Suppose (
44
) holds for
K\in\mathbb{N}
, namely, for all
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
,
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|\longrightarrow 0,\ \ \textnormal{as }N\to\infty.

We need to show that for all
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K+1})
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{K+1}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K+1}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|\longrightarrow 0,\ \ \textnormal{as }N\to\infty.

Let
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K+1})
. We have by definition that

\displaystyle\tilde{\Lambda}_{K+1}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K+1}}\det\left(\phi_{\omega\left(x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N-K-j+1}^{(N)}\right)}^{(j-1)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{i}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{K+1}\Delta_{K+1}(\mathbf{y})g(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K+1}}\det\left(\phi_{\omega\left(x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)}^{(K+1-i)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{K+1}\Delta_{K+1}(\mathbf{y})g(\mathbf{y}).

Also, from (
58
) we have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K+1}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)=\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K+1}}\det\left(\mathcal{M}^{(K+1-i)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j};x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{K+1}\Delta_{K+1}(\mathbf{y})g(\mathbf{y}).

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\tilde{\Lambda}_{K+1}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K+1}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\Big|=\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K+1}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\Bigg[\det\left(\phi_{\omega\left(x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)}^{(K+1-i)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{K+1}

\displaystyle\hskip 123.76965pt-\det\left(\mathcal{M}^{(K+1-i)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j};x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{K+1}\Bigg]\Delta_{K+1}(\mathbf{y})g(\mathbf{y})\Bigg|

Now, by expanding both determinants along the first row (recall the definition of the determinant in the weak sense is consistent with expanding along a row or column), we get

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\tilde{\Lambda}_{K+1}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K+1}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\Big|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K+1}}\sum_{k=1}^{K+1}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K}\frac{1}{l!}\right)(-1)^{1+k}\Bigg[\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}^{(K)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k})\det\left(\phi_{\omega\left(x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)}^{(K+1-i)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j})\right)_{i=2,j=1,j\neq k}^{K+1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\mathcal{M}^{(K)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k};\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\det\left(\mathcal{M}^{(K+1-i)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j};x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)\right)_{i=2,j=1,j\neq k}^{K+1}\Bigg]\Delta_{K+1}(\mathbf{y})g(\mathbf{y})\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K!}\Bigg|\sum_{k=1}^{K+1}(-1)^{1+k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K-1}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\Bigg[\det\left(\phi_{\omega\left(x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)}^{(K+1-i)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j})\right)_{i=2,j=1,j\neq k}^{K+1}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-\det\left(\mathcal{M}^{(K+1-i)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j};x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)\right)_{i=2,j=1,j\neq k}^{K+1}\Bigg]\prod_{\underset{i,j\neq K+2-k}{1\leq i<j\leq K+1}}(y_{i}-y_{j})

\displaystyle\hskip 136.5733pt\times(-1)^{K+1-k}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}^{(K)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k})\prod_{\underset{j\neq K+2-k}{1\leq j\leq K+1}}(y_{K+2-k}-y_{j})g(\mathbf{y})}_{(-1)^{K}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k})h_{k}(\mathbf{y})}

\displaystyle\;\;\;+\sum_{k=1}^{K+1}(-1)^{1+k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\det\left(\mathcal{M}^{(K+1-i)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j};x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)\right)_{\underset{j\neq k}{i=2,j=1}}^{K+1}\prod_{\underset{i,j\neq K+2-k}{1\leq i<j\leq K+1}}(y_{i}-y_{j})

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt\times(-1)^{K+1-k}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\mathcal{M}^{(K)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k};\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)-\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}^{(K)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k})\right]\prod_{\underset{j\neq K+2-k}{1\leq j\leq K+1}}(y_{K+2-k}-y_{j})g(\mathbf{y})}_{(-1)^{K}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\mathcal{M}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k};\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)-\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k})\right]h_{k}(\mathbf{y})}\Bigg|,

where
h_{k}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K+1})
is defined by

\displaystyle h_{k}(\mathbf{y})=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{K}}{\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k}^{K}}\left[\prod_{\underset{j\neq K+2-k}{1\leq j\leq K+1}}(y_{K+2-k}-y_{j})g(\mathbf{y})\right].

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\tilde{\Lambda}_{K+1}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K+1}^{N}g\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\Big|\leq\frac{1}{K!}\sum_{k=1}^{K+1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k})

\displaystyle\quad\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K-1}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\Bigg[\det\left(\phi_{\omega\left(x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)}^{(K+1-i)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j})\right)_{i=2,j=1,j\neq k}^{K+1}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\det\left(\mathcal{M}^{(K+1-i)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j};x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)\right)_{i=2,j=1,j\neq k}^{K+1}\Bigg]\prod_{\underset{i,j\neq K+2-k}{1\leq i<j\leq K+1}}(y_{i}-y_{j})h_{k}(\mathbf{y})\Bigg|

\displaystyle\ \ +\frac{1}{K!}\sum_{k=1}^{K+1}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\det\left(\mathcal{M}^{(K+1-i)}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-j};x_{1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N+1-i}^{(N)}\right)\right)_{i=2,j=1,j\neq k}^{K+1}\prod_{\underset{i,j\neq K+2-k}{1\leq i<j\leq K+1}}(y_{i}-y_{j})

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt\times\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\mathcal{M}\left(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k};\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)-\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+2-k})\right]h_{k}(\mathbf{y})\right|.

To complete the proof, we need to show that
the above expression becomes arbitrarily small, uniformly in
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}
, for large enough
N
.
Clearly, it suffices to prove this for each term of the above two series individually. We prove that this is indeed the case for the first series. A completely analogous argument works for the second too.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be given.
Fix
1\leq k\leq K+1
and consider the
k
-th term of the first series. For each
a\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathsf{h}_{a}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
be the function defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}_{a}(\mathbf{y})\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}h_{k}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k-1},a,y_{k},\dots,y_{K}),\ \ \mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}.

Since
h_{k}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K+1})
, there exists a compact interval
\mathfrak{I}_{k}
such that
\mathsf{h}_{a}\equiv 0

for all
a\notin\mathfrak{I}_{k}
.
Moreover, by virtue of the induction hypothesis, for every
a\in\mathfrak{I}_{k}
, there exist a smallest
\mathsf{N}(a)\in\mathbb{N}
(depending on
a
), such that for all
N\geq\mathsf{N}(a)
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}\mathsf{h}_{a}\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K}^{N}\mathsf{h}_{a}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|<\varepsilon.

(72)

Note now that if
\mathsf{M}_{k}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\sup_{a\in\mathfrak{I}_{k}}\mathsf{N}(a)
is finite,
then the above inequality holds for all
a\in\mathfrak{I}_{k}
and
N\geq\mathsf{M}_{k}
, and so, the result follows immediately owing to the fact that
\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
for every
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
.

We thus wish to show that
\mathsf{M}_{k}<\infty
. Suppose by contradiction that it is not true. Accordingly, one can find a sequence
\left(\mathfrak{a}_{n}\right)_{n=1}^{\infty}\in\mathfrak{I}_{k}
with
\mathfrak{a}_{n}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\mathfrak{a}_{*}\in\mathfrak{I}_{k}
such that

\mathsf{N}(n)\nearrow\infty
.
Let
\mathsf{h}_{*}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathsf{h}_{\mathfrak{a}_{*}}
and

\mathsf{N}_{*}\in\mathbb{N}

be such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}\mathsf{h}_{*}\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K}^{N}\mathsf{h}_{*}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

for all
N\geq\mathsf{N}_{*}
(note that
\mathsf{N}_{*}
is also well-defined by the induction hypothesis).

Now, since
h_{k}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K+1})
and
\mathfrak{a}_{n}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\mathfrak{a}_{*}
, we have
\mathsf{h}_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathsf{h}_{\mathfrak{a}_{n}}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{h}_{*}
pointwise. Moreover,
it follows from the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem that there exists a uniformly convergent sub-sequence of this sequence which we denote by the same notation.
Hence, we can pick
\mathsf{m}\in\mathbb{N}
large enough so that for all
n\geq\mathsf{m}
, we have
\mathsf{N}(n)>\mathsf{N}_{*}
and

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{h}_{n}(\mathbf{y})-\mathsf{h}_{*}(\mathbf{y})\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{4},\ \ \forall\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}\mathsf{h}_{\mathsf{m}}\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K}^{N}\mathsf{h}_{\mathsf{m}}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}(\mathsf{h}_{\mathsf{m}}-\mathsf{h}_{*})\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)\right|+\left|\Lambda_{K}^{N}(\mathsf{h}_{\mathsf{m}}-\mathsf{h}_{*})\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\ \ +\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}\mathsf{h}_{*}\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K}^{N}\mathsf{h}_{*}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle<\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}=\varepsilon,

for all
N\geq\mathsf{N}_{*}
, and
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
. This implies that

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}\mathsf{h}_{\mathsf{m}}\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K}^{N}\mathsf{h}_{\mathsf{m}}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right|<\varepsilon,\ \ \forall N\geq\mathsf{N}_{*}.

Thus, we must have
\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{m})\leq\mathsf{N}_{*}
which is a contradiction in view of the particular choice of
\mathsf{N}(\mathsf{m})
and
\mathsf{m}
.
∎

We finally conclude Theorem
2.23
by proving the following proposition.

Proposition 2.43
.

Let
K\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, for all
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{K})
we have:

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)\right|\to 0,\;\;\textnormal{as }N\to\infty.

Proof.

We have by definition

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\left|\tilde{\Lambda}_{K}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\Lambda_{K}^{\infty}g\left(\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\;=\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\Bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K-1}\frac{1}{l!}\right)\left(\det\left(\phi_{\omega\left(x_{K-j+1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N}^{(N)}\right)}^{(j-1)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+1-i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{K}-\det\left(\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}^{(j-1)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+1-i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{K}\right)h\left(\mathbf{y}\right)\Bigg|,

(73)

with

h(\mathbf{y})=\Delta_{K}(\mathbf{y})g(\mathbf{y})
.
We show that the above expression is arbitrarily small for large enough
N
. Define the following
K\times K
matrices,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{F}}\left(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)=\begin{bmatrix}(-\textnormal{i}z_{K})^{K-1}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{K}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N}\right)^{-1}&\dots&(-\textnormal{i}z_{1})^{K-1}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{1}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N}\right)^{-1}\\
(-\textnormal{i}z_{K})^{K-2}\prod_{j=2}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{K}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N-1}\right)^{-1}&\dots&(-\textnormal{i}z_{1})^{K-2}\prod_{j=2}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{1}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N-1}\right)^{-1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\prod_{j=K}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{K}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N-K+1}\right)^{-1}&\dots&\prod_{j=K}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{1}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N-K+1}\right)^{-1}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}\left(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)=\begin{bmatrix}(-\textnormal{i}z_{K})^{K-1}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{K}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N}\right)^{-1}&\dots&(-\textnormal{i}z_{1})^{K-1}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{1}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N}\right)^{-1}\\
(-\textnormal{i}z_{K})^{K-2}\prod_{j=2}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{K}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N}\right)^{-1}&\dots&(-\textnormal{i}z_{1})^{K-2}\prod_{j=2}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{1}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N}\right)^{-1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\prod_{j=K}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{K}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N}\right)^{-1}&\dots&\prod_{j=K}^{N}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{1}x_{j}^{(N)}}{N}\right)^{-1}\end{bmatrix}.

We now use (
68
) repeatedly to rewrite the determinants in (
73
) in terms of the determinants of the corresponding Fourier transforms.
Then, we do elementary row operations repeatedly for the second determinant.
We finally use (
69
) and expand both determinants in order to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}

\displaystyle\left[\det\left(\phi_{\omega\left(x_{K-j+1}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N}^{(N)}\right)}^{(j-1)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+1-i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{K}-\det\left(\phi_{\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)}^{(j-1)}(\mathrm{d}y_{K+1-i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{K}\right]\Delta_{K}(\mathbf{y})g\left(\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-K}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\left[\det\left(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}\left(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)-\det\left(\mathbf{F}\left(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right)\right]\check{h}\left(\mathbf{z}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-K}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}\textnormal{sgn}(\sigma)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{K}}\left[\prod_{l=1}^{K}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{\sigma(l)}w_{\sigma(l)}}{N-l+1}\right)^{-(N-l+1)}-\prod_{l=1}^{K}\left(1-\frac{\textnormal{i}z_{\sigma(l)}w_{\sigma(l)}}{N}\right)^{-(N-l+1)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\times\left(\prod_{l=1}^{K}(-\textnormal{i}z_{\sigma(l)})^{K-l}\check{h}(\mathbf{z})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}\right)\prod_{l=1}^{K}\mathcal{M}\left(\mathrm{d}w_{\sigma(l)};x_{l}^{(N)},\dots,x_{N}^{(N)}\right).

Note now that for any fixed
w_{\sigma(l)}
, the expression in the brackets on the RHS goes to
0
pointwise in
z_{\sigma(l)}
, and moreover, it is bounded. Therefore, since the function in the parentheses is integrable, the inner integral converges to
0
by the dominated convergence theorem. One can then use a similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
2.42
to verify that the convergence is indeed uniform and hence, the expression can be made arbitrarily small by picking large enough
N
. Finally, the conclusion follows by fact that
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{d}w;\mathbf{x})
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
.
∎

3

Properties of the finite-dimensional diffusions

3.1

Well-posedness and Feller property

In this subsection we present several, for the most part standard, results on the dynamics (
4
) that we will need.

Lemma 3.1
.

The SDE (
4
) has a unique strong non-exploding solution
\left(\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
for any initial condition
\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(0)=\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
. Moreover, almost surely, for all
t>0
,
\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t)\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{>}
, where

\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{>}=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:x_{1}>x_{2}>\cdots>x_{N}\geq 0\right\}.

Finally, if
\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(0)=\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{\circ}
, then almost surely for all
t\geq 0
,
\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t)\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{\circ}
.

Proof.

All statements, except the last one, follow from the main theorem of
[
45
]
by verifying the elementary conditions therein. To establish the last statement, observe that since
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{\circ}
, using Itô’s formula we have, for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\log\mathfrak{x}_{N}^{(N)}(t)

\displaystyle=\log x_{N}+\mathsf{w}_{N}(t)-\frac{1+\eta}{2}t+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2\mathfrak{x}_{N}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}\frac{\mathfrak{x}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}{\mathfrak{x}_{N}^{(N)}(s)-\mathfrak{x}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\geq\log x_{N}+\mathsf{w}_{N}(t)-\frac{1+\eta}{2}t-(N-1)t\left(\inf_{s\in[0,t]}\left|1-\frac{\mathfrak{x}_{N}^{(N)}(s)}{\mathfrak{x}_{N-1}^{(N)}(s)}\right|\right)^{-1}.

The conclusion then follows since the RHS is finite.
∎

The Markov semigroup associated with
\left(\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
, the unique strong solution of (
4
), is denoted by
\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
.

We next introduce certain matrix-valued dynamics
\left(\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)};t\geq 0\right)
from
[
96
]
whose eigenvalue evolution is described by the SDE (
4
).
Recall that
\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
denotes the space of
N\times N
non-negative definite Hermitian matrices.
We denote by
\textnormal{Tr}(\mathbf{A})
the trace of a matrix
\mathbf{A}
. Consider the following
\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
-valued SDE introduced by Rider-Valko in
[
96
]
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}^{(N)}\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)}+\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)}\mathrm{d}\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}^{(N)}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)+\left(-\frac{\eta+N}{2}\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)}+\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\textnormal{Tr}\left(\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)}\right)\right)\mathbf{I}\right)\mathrm{d}t,

(74)

where

\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{t}^{(N)};t\geq 0\right)
is a matrix-valued complex Brownian motion; namely, an
N\times N
matrix with the entries independent complex Brownian motions (the real and imaginary parts are independent standard real Brownian motions), and

\mathbf{I}
is the
N\times N
identity matrix.

Lemma 3.2
.

For any
\mathbf{H}_{0}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
, the SDE (
74
) has a unique strong solution.

Proof.

It is easy to see that the coefficients of the equation are Lipschitz continuous in matrix norm, and thus existence and uniqueness of the strong solution follows from well-known results, see for example
[
44
]
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
and
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
be such that
\mathsf{eval}_{N}\left(\mathbf{H}\right)=\mathbf{x}
.
Let
\left(\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)};t\geq 0\right)
be the solution to (
74
) starting from
\mathbf{H}
and
\left(\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
be the solution to (
4
) starting from
\mathbf{x}
. Then,

\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N}\left(\mathbf{H}^{(N)}_{t}\right);t\geq 0\right)\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\left(\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0\right).

(75)

Proof.

The statement is a consequence of the general result of
[
44
]
, which relates matrix diffusions solving SDE generalising (
74
) to their eigenvalues evolutions given in terms of SDE generalising (
4
). The computation is also implicit in
[
96
]
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

By looking at the stochastic equation satisfied by the process
t\mapsto\det\big(\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)}\big)
, see proof of Proposition 12 in

[
96
]
, we can improve the statement of Lemma
3.1
regarding never reaching the origin, to initial conditions
\mathbf{x}
with coinciding coordinates.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. The semigroup
\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is a Feller semigroup.

Proof.

The semigroup associated to
\left(\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)};t\geq 0\right)
, as a unique strong solution to a SDE with Lipschitz coefficients, is Feller by classical results, see
[
95
]
. The Feller property of the eigenvalue process follows from that of the matrix process. More precisely,
let
\left(\mathsf{Q}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
denote the semigroup associated to
\left(\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)};t\geq 0\right)
. Note that, if
\mathbf{x}=\mathsf{eval}_{N}(\mathbf{H})
, one has from Lemma
3.3
,

\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)f\right)(\mathbf{x})=\left(\mathsf{Q}_{N}(t)f\circ\mathsf{eval}_{N}\right)\left(\mathsf{diag}(\mathbf{x})\right),

and moreover, note that if
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
then
f\circ\mathsf{eval}_{N}\in C_{0}(\mathbb{H}_{+}(N))
. From this, and the Feller property of
\left(\mathsf{Q}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
, one immediately checks the Feller property for
\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

By hypoellipticity
[
102
]
of the generator of
(\mathsf{Q}_{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
, shown in Proposition 12 of
[
96
]
, we have that
\mathsf{Q}_{N}(t)(\mathbf{A},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B})=\mathsf{Q}_{N}(t)(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}
, with
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}
Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
, where the density
\mathsf{Q}_{N}(t)(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is smooth, viewed as a function in the matrix entries, see
[
96
]
.

Our next goal is to give a description of the
N
-dimensional dynamics
\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
, in some sense, in terms of one-dimensional diffusions. For this, we define for fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
, consider unique strong solution to the following SDE in
[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathsf{z}^{(N)}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{z}^{(N)}(t)\mathrm{d}\mathsf{w}(t)+\left[\left(1-\frac{\eta}{2}-N\right)\mathsf{z}^{(N)}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\right]\mathrm{d}t,

(76)

with
\mathsf{w}
as a usual a standard Brownian motion, with infinitesimal generator,

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{x}^{(N)}=\frac{x^{2}}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}+\left(\left(1-\frac{\eta}{2}-N\right)x+\frac{1}{2}\right)\partial_{x}

(77)

and transition density with respect to Lebesgue measure in
[0,\infty)
that we denote by
\mathfrak{p}^{(N)}_{t}(x,y)
. It is known that
\infty
is a natural boundary and
0
an entrance boundary in the terminology of Feller
[
46
,
34
]
. In particular, almost surely, for all strictly positive times the diffusion
\mathsf{z}^{(N)}
is in
(0,\infty)
.

We then have the following proposition. Analogous results are well-known for certain generalisations of Dyson Brownian motion: the radial Dunkl
[
98
]
and Heckman-Opdam
[
100
]
processes.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,
C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}\right)
forms a core for the generator of the semigroup
\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
