and it is moreover invariant under the action of the generator. Finally, the kernel
\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})
of
\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)
has an explicit density with respect to the Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
given by (abusing notation), for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{\circ}
,
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)(x,y)=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{N}t}\frac{\Delta_{N}(\mathbf{y})}{\Delta_{N}(\mathbf{x})}\det\left(\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}(x_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N},

(78)

with
\lambda_{N}=N(N-1)\left(1-\frac{3\eta}{2}-2N\right)/6
.

Proof.

Let us denote by
(\mathsf{L}_{N},\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{L}_{N}))
the generator of
(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
. Define the formal differential operator
\mathbf{L}_{N}
by,

\mathbf{L}_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{x_{i}^{2}}{2}\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[-\frac{\eta}{2}x_{i}+\frac{1}{2}+\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\right]\partial_{x_{i}}.

(79)

We will identify shortly
\mathsf{L}_{N}
with
\mathbf{L}_{N}
on
C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}\right)
.

We first check that
C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
is invariant under
\mathbf{L}_{N}
. Let
g\in C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
be arbitrary and let
f
be a function in
C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
that restricts to
g\in C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
. Consider
\mathbf{L}_{N}f
, where we view
\mathbf{L}_{N}
as acting on functions on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
in the obvious way, and observe that on
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
this restricts to
\mathbf{L}_{N}g
. It then suffices to show
\mathbf{L}_{N}f\in C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
.
\mathbf{L}_{N}f
is clearly of compact support and moreover it is symmetric in its arguments. Showing smoothness boils down to showing that the only part which is possibly singular,

(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\mapsto\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}x_{i}x_{j}\frac{\partial_{x_{i}}f(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})-\partial_{x_{j}}f(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})}{x_{i}-x_{j}},

is actually smooth on the whole of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. This moreover boils down to proving the following claim. For a smooth symmetric function
\mathrm{H}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the set of which we denote by
C_{\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the function

(x,y)\mapsto\frac{\partial_{x}\mathrm{H}(x,y)-\partial_{y}\mathrm{H}(x,y)}{x-y}

is smooth on the whole of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, which is a direct consequence of known results. Namely, for
\mathrm{H}\in C_{\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
let
\mathfrak{H}
be the unique function such that

\mathrm{H}(x,y)=\mathfrak{H}\left(\mathrm{S}_{1}(x,y),\mathrm{S}_{2}(x,y)\right),

where
\mathrm{S}_{1}(x,y)=-(x+y)
and
\mathrm{S}_{2}(x,y)=xy
, see
[
12
]
.
Then, we have, see
[
12
]
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{x}\mathrm{H}(x,y)-\partial_{y}\mathrm{H}(x,y)}{x-y}=-\frac{\partial\mathfrak{H}}{\partial\mathrm{S}_{2}}.

Finally, from Corollary 3.3 of
[
12
]
,
\mathfrak{H}
is a smooth function and this establishes the claim.

We now show that
\mathsf{L}_{N}
extends
\mathbf{L}_{N}
on
C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
and
C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
is a core for it. First, for
f
so that
\mathbf{L}_{N}f
makes sense, define the process
t\mapsto\mathsf{M}_{f}(t)
by,

\mathsf{M}_{f}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}f(\mathsf{z}(t))-f(\mathsf{z}(0))-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{L}_{N}f(\mathsf{z}(s))\mathrm{d}s,

(80)

where
\mathsf{Law}(\mathsf{z})=\mathsf{Law}(\mathfrak{x})
, with
\mathfrak{x}
the unique strong solution to (
4
), namely the process with generator
\mathsf{L}_{N}
. Observe that, for
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
,
\mathsf{M}_{f}
is bounded since
\mathbf{L}_{N}C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})\subset C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
. Hence, an application of Itô’s formula
[
95
,
51
]
to the solution of the SDE (
4
) gives that
t\mapsto\mathsf{M}_{f}(t)
is a martingale (with respect to the natural filtration of the coordinate process). In particular, from Theorem 17.23 of
[
51
]
we obtain that
\mathsf{L}_{N}
extends (we do not know yet if this extension is unique)
\mathbf{L}_{N}\big|_{C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})}
to
C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
.

It remains to show that
C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
is actually a core for the generator
\mathsf{L}_{N}
. This is done by connecting the SDE (
4
) to a well-posed martingale problem. Define
C_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{W}_{N,+})=\left\{f\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{W}_{N,+}):f(0)=\mathbf{x}\right\}
.
We say that a family of probability measures
\{\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\}_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}}
on
C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
, endowed with the filtration generated by the coordinate process, is a solution to the martingale problem for
(\mathbf{L}_{N},C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}))
if
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}(C_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{W}_{N,+}))=1
, and under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}
, for every
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
, the process
t\mapsto\mathsf{M}_{f}(t)
defined in (
80
), where
\mathsf{Law}(\mathsf{z})=\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}
,
is a martingale. We now show that this martingale problem is well-posed, namely has a unique solution. First, the law of any weak solution, to the SDE (
4
) starting from
\mathbf{x}
, gives rises to a solution to the martingale problem by an application of Itô’s formula
[
95
,
51
]
. We then show that this solution to the martingale problem must be unique. Consider an arbitrary solution to the martingale problem. For
R>0
, define the stopping times,

\zeta_{R}=\inf\left\{t\geq 0;||\mathsf{z}(t)||\geq R\ \ \textnormal{or}\ \sum_{1=i\neq j=N}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathsf{z}_{i}(s)\mathsf{z}_{j}(s)}{\mathsf{z}_{i}(s)-\mathsf{z}_{j}(s)}\mathrm{d}s\right|\geq R\right\},

where, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
||\mathbf{x}||
is its Euclidean norm. For
i,j=1,\dots,N
, we choose
\mathrm{f}_{i},\mathrm{f}_{ij}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
satisfying
\mathrm{f}_{i}(\mathbf{x})=x_{i},\mathrm{f}_{ij}(\mathbf{x})=x_{i}x_{j}
, for
||\mathbf{x}||\leq R
. We now claim that, for all
i,j=1,\dots,N
, the processes defined by,

t\mapsto\mathsf{N}_{i}(t\wedge\zeta_{R})\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathsf{M}_{\mathrm{f}_{i}^{R}}(t\wedge\zeta_{R})=\mathsf{z}_{i}(t\wedge\zeta_{R})-\mathsf{z}_{i}(0)-\int_{0}^{t\wedge\eta_{R}}\left(-\frac{\eta}{2}\mathsf{z}_{i}(s)+\frac{1}{2}+\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\frac{\mathsf{z}_{i}(s)\mathsf{z}_{j}(s)}{\mathsf{z}_{i}(s)-\mathsf{z}_{j}(s)}\right)\mathrm{d}s

and
t\mapsto\mathsf{N}_{ij}(t\wedge\zeta_{R})\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathsf{M}_{\mathrm{f}_{ij}^{R}}(t\wedge\zeta_{R})
are martingales. We approximate
\mathrm{f}_{i}^{R}
and
\mathrm{f}_{ij}^{R}
by functions in
C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
, for which we know the martingale property of
\mathsf{M}_{f}
. By the Stone-Weirstrass theorem the space
C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
is dense in
C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
but we need slightly more in convergence of the derivatives.
More generally, let us take a function
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N})
(note that we can view the functions
\mathrm{f}_{i}^{R},\mathrm{f}_{ij}^{R}
as in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N})
). Then, extend
g
by symmetry to the whole of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
to obtain a function
\mathsf{G}
. Observe that, by construction
\mathsf{G}
is symmetric, has compact support, is smooth except on the hyperplanes
\{x_{i_{1}}=x_{i_{2}}=\cdots=x_{i_{\ell}}\}
, is Lipschitz on the whole of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
\mathsf{G}|_{\mathbb{W}_{N}}=g
. Let
\mathfrak{u}
be a symmetric approximation of the identity and define, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathfrak{g}_{m}(\mathbf{x})\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathsf{G}(\mathbf{y})m^{N}\mathfrak{u}\left(m(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}.

Then, observe that,
\mathfrak{g}_{m}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and that
\mathfrak{g}_{m}
approximates, as
m\to\infty
,
g
in
C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N})
and all its derivatives approximate uniformly on compact sets in
\mathbb{W}_{N}^{\circ}
the derivatives of
g
. From the above we get that there exist sequences
(\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{m})_{m=1}^{\infty},(\mathfrak{f}_{ij}^{m})_{m=1}^{\infty}
in
C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
so that, as
m\to\infty
, for all
i,j=1,\dots,N
,

\mathsf{M}_{\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{m}}(\cdot\wedge\zeta_{R})\longrightarrow\mathsf{M}_{\mathrm{f}_{i}^{R}}(\cdot\wedge\zeta_{R}),\ \mathsf{M}_{\mathfrak{f}_{ij}^{m}}(\cdot\wedge\zeta_{R})\longrightarrow\mathsf{M}_{\mathrm{f}_{ij}^{R}}(\cdot\wedge\zeta_{R}),

uniformly on compact sets, in distribution under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}
. Hence, we easily obtain that for all
i,j=1,\dots,N
, the processes
t\mapsto\mathsf{M}_{\mathrm{f}_{i}^{R}}(t\wedge\zeta_{R})
,
t\mapsto\mathsf{M}_{\mathrm{f}_{ij}^{R}}(t\wedge\zeta_{R})
are martingales as desired. Then, we can follow the computation in Theorem 32.7 of
[
51
]
or Proposition 5.4.6 of
[
52
]
to obtain independent standard Brownian motions
(\mathsf{w}_{i}(\cdot))_{i=1}^{N}
(with respect to possibly an extension of the original filtration), such that, for all
i=1,\dots,N
,

\mathsf{N}_{i}(t\wedge\zeta_{R})=\int_{0}^{t\wedge\zeta_{R}}\mathsf{z}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}\mathsf{w}_{i}(s),\ \ \forall t\geq 0.

In particular, we obtain that
(\mathsf{z}_{i}(\cdot\wedge\zeta_{R}),\mathsf{w}_{i}(\cdot\wedge\zeta_{R}))_{i=1}^{N}
is a weak solution to the SDE (
4
) up until time
\zeta_{R}
. But by pathwise uniqueness shown in
[
45
]
, this solution must be equal to the unique strong solution of (
4
) up until
\zeta_{R}
. Observe that, under the law of the solution to (
4
),
\zeta_{R}\to\infty
, as
R\to\infty
. In particular,
\zeta_{R}\to\infty
, as
R\to\infty
, under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}
and from a solution to the martingale problem we constructed a solution to (
4
) for all times. Hence, the solution to the martingale problem for
(\mathbf{L}_{N},C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}))
must be unique (for otherwise we would have constructed two different in law solutions to (
4
)). The associated diffusion process to it, see
[
51
]
, is clearly nothing but the solution to the SDE (
4
), namely the Feller process with semigroup
(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
. By virtue of Theorem 2.5 of
[
110
]
, since
C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
is dense
C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
and the martingale problem for
(\mathbf{L}_{N},C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}))
is well-posed, we obtain that operator
\mathbf{L}_{N}
is closable in
C_{0}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
and the closure is nothing but the generator
\mathsf{L}_{N}
of the semigroup
(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
. Namely
\overline{\mathsf{L}_{N}|_{C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})}}=\overline{\mathbf{L}_{N}|_{C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})}}=\mathsf{L}_{N}
or in other words,
C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
is a core for
(\mathsf{L}_{N},\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{L}_{N}))
.

The final statement in the proposition is a direct consequence of the following observation. The generator
\mathbf{L}_{N}
acting on
g\in C_{c,\textnormal{sym}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
can be written, after an elementary computation, as

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{N}g(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=\left[\Delta_{N}(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\circ\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{L}_{x_{i}}^{(N)}\right)\circ\Delta_{N}(\mathbf{x})-\lambda_{N}\right]g(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}),

which is exactly the Doob
h
-transform, see
[
95
,
90
,
29
]
, of
N
independent one-dimensional diffusions with generator
\mathsf{L}^{(N)}
killed when they intersect, by the Vandermonde determinant
\Delta_{N}
. This is the Markov process which has transition kernel having density (
78
), by virtue of the Karlin-McGregor formula
[
54
,
46
]
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

For
N=1
, the fact that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is a core for the
\mathsf{L}^{(1)}
-diffusion is well-known and a very special case of general results, see Chapter 8 of
[
34
]
.

3.2

Consistency

The following consistency or intertwining relation between
\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
and
\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N+1}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is the main algebraic ingredient for the method of intertwiners.

Theorem 3.9
.

We have the intertwining relations:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{N+1}(t)\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}=\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t),\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\ \forall N\in\mathbb{N}.

(81)

We present two arguments for a proof. The first argument is a little more sophisticated and uses matrix stochastic calculus. It is conceptually more natural as it somehow explains the origin of the intertwining: the intertwining (
81
) simply follows from consistency with respect to corners projection at the level of the matrix diffusions. On the other hand, we only give a full proof for the special initial condition
\mathbf{H}^{(N)}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
which allows for important simplifications (in the case of Dyson Brownian motion which is also consistent with
\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}
the adaptation of the argument works for any initial condition). The second argument is a direct verification, which works for all initial conditions, and thus is a complete proof, using the explicit formula from (
78
).

We begin with a little formalism giving consistency at the level of the eigenvalues from consistency at the level of the matrix process.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\left(\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(N)};t\geq 0\right)
and
\left(\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(N+1)};t\geq 0\right)
be stochastic processes on
\mathbb{H}(N)
and
\mathbb{H}(N+1)
respectively. Suppose that
\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N}\left(\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(N)}\right);t\geq 0\right)
and
\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N+1}\left(\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(N+1)}\right);t\geq 0\right)
are Markovian with semigroups
\left(\mathsf{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
and
\left(\mathsf{P}_{N+1}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
respectively. Assume that, for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{U}^{(K)}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(K)}\mathbf{U}^{(K)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(K)},\ \ \forall\mathbf{U}^{(K)}\in\mathbb{U}(K),\ \ K=N,N+1.

(82)

Suppose moreover that there exists a set
\mathscr{A}\subseteq\mathbb{H}(N+1)
such that for all
\mathbf{C}_{0}^{(N+1)}\in\mathscr{A}
,

\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(N+1)}\right)\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(N)},\ \ \forall t\geq 0.

(83)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{N+1}(t)\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}(\mathbf{x},\cdot)=\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\mathsf{P}_{N}(t)(\mathbf{x},\cdot),\ \ \forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{eval}_{N+1}(\mathscr{A}),\ \ \forall t\geq 0.

(84)

Proof.

Fix
\mathbf{x}
so that
\mathbf{x}=\mathsf{eval}_{(N+1)}\left(\mathbf{C}_{0}^{(N+1)}\right)
, with
\mathbf{C}_{0}^{(N+1)}\in\mathscr{A}
. We claim that, the LHS of (
84
) is given by,

\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N}\left(\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(N+1)}\right)\right)\right),

while the RHS by,

\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N}\left(\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(N)}\right)\right).

These are equal by virtue of (
83
) and the conclusion follows. To see the claim, first observe that,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N}\left(\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(N)}\right)\right)(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})=\mu\mathsf{P}_{N}(t)(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}),

where, by unitary invariance (
82
),
\mu
is given by,

\mu(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N}\left(\mathbf{C}_{0}^{(N)}\right)\right)(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})=\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N}\left(\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{C}_{0}^{(N+1)}\right)\right)\right)(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})=\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\right).

On the other hand, again by unitary invariance (
82
), we also have,

\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N}\left(\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(N+1)}\right)\right)\right)(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})=\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N+1}\left(\mathbf{C}_{t}^{(N+1)}\right)\right)\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})=\nu\mathsf{P}_{N+1}(t)\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}),

where
\nu
is given by,

\nu(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N+1}\left(\mathbf{C}_{0}^{(N+1)}\right)\right)=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}).

This completes the proof.
∎

We will apply this proposition (with the obvious notational identifications) to prove Theorem
3.9
in the special case
\mathbf{H}_{0}^{(N)}=\mathbf{0}
, namely for
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}
.

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
\left(\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)};t\geq 0\right)
be the unique strong solution of the equation (
74
) starting from
\mathbf{H}_{0}^{(N)}=\mathbf{0}
, where
\mathbf{0}
denotes
N\times N
zero matrix. We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N+1)}\right),\ \ \forall t\geq 0.

(85)

We first show that the solution to the SDE (
74
) can be solved explicitly in terms of the matrix exponential Brownian motion
\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)};t\geq 0\right)
, which we introduce next. This is given by the unique strong solution of the matrix SDE:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N)}-\theta\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}\mathrm{d}t,

(86)

where
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and
\left(\mathbf{W}^{(N)}_{t};t\geq 0\right)
is an
N\times N
complex matrix-valued Brownian motion. Below we use the notation
\mathbf{A}^{-{\dagger}}
for
\left(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger}\right)^{-1}
.

Proposition 3.12
.

The unique strong solution
\left(\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)};t\geq 0\right)
of (
74
), starting from
\mathbf{H}^{(N)}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
is given by

\displaystyle\left(\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(-\nu_{N})}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{H}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(-\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(-\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}s\right)\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(-\nu_{N})}\right)^{-{\dagger}};t\geq 0\right).

(87)

where
\nu_{N}=\frac{1}{4}(\eta+N)
, with
\eta
being the same parameter as throughout this paper.

Proof.

First, note that from (
86
) we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}\right)^{{\dagger}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}\left(\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N)}\right)^{{\dagger}}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}\right)^{{\dagger}}-\theta\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}t.

Moreover, using Itô’s formula we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N)}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}\right)^{-1}+\theta\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}\right)^{-1}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}\right)^{-{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}\right)^{-{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}\left(\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N)}\right)^{{\dagger}}+\theta\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\theta)}\right)^{-{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}t.

One can then check directly that (
87
) solves the SDE (
74
) by applying Itô’s formula to the expression in (
87
) and using the equations above.
∎

Proposition 3.13
.

In the setting of Theorem
3.11
, we have, for fixed
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}s,

(88)

where
\nu_{N}=\frac{1}{4}(\eta+N)
, with
\eta
being the same parameter as throughout this paper.

Proof.

For any fixed
t\geq 0
, define

\left(\mathbf{N}_{s}^{(\nu_{N})};0\leq s\leq t\right)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left(\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(-\nu_{N})}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{M}_{t-s}^{(-\nu_{N})};0\leq s\leq t\right).

Then, making the change of variable
u\to t-u
in (
87
), one can write

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(-\nu_{N})}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{0}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(-\nu_{N})}\right)^{-{\dagger}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{N}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{N}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}u.

(89)

From (
89
), we have when
\mathbf{H}_{0}^{(N)}=\mathbf{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{N}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{N}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}u.

(90)

Moreover, it is shown in Lemma 2.1 of
[
6
]
(essentially the matrix analogue of the time-reversal property of Brownian motion) that,

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{N}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})};0\leq u\leq t\right)\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\left(\mathbf{M}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})};0\leq u\leq t\right),

which implies the result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.11
.

By virtue of (
88
), it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}s\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}s\right),\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\ \ N\in\mathbb{N}.

By applying Itô’s formula we have from (
86
) with
\theta=\nu_{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}+\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\mathrm{d}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}+\mathrm{d}\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\mathrm{d}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N)}+\mathrm{d}\left(\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N)}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}+\left(\frac{N}{2}-2\nu_{N}\right)\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}t.

(91)

Now, using Lévy’s characterization theorem, see
[
95
]
, one
can easily check that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{t}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N)}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(92)

is an
N\times N
matrix complex Brownian motion. Thus, the equation (
91
) can be written in the following closed form:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)}\left(\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{t}^{(N)}+\mathrm{d}\left(\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{t}^{(N)}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)\sqrt{\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}}

\displaystyle\ \ +\left(\frac{N}{2}-2\nu_{N}\right)\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}t.

(93)

We next consider the SDE for the
(N+1)\times(N+1)
matrix process, that we can write as follows

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N+1)}-\nu_{N+1}\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\\
\mathbf{b}_{t}^{\textnormal{T}}&c_{t}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{W}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{u}_{t}\\
\mathbf{v}_{t}^{\textnormal{T}}&w_{t}\end{pmatrix}-\nu_{N+1}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\\
\mathbf{b}_{t}^{\textnormal{T}}&c_{t}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}t,

(94)

where
\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}
is the
N\times N
top-left corner of
\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}
,
\mathbf{a}_{t}
and
\mathbf{b}_{t}
are

\mathbb{C}^{N}
-valued, and
c_{t}\in\mathbb{C}
. Here,

\tilde{\mathbf{W}}_{t}^{(N)}
,
\mathbf{u}_{t},\mathbf{v}_{t}
, and
w_{t}
are complex matrix, complex vector, and
\mathbb{C}
-valued Brownian motions and
\mathbf{A}^{\textnormal{T}}
denotes the transpose of
\mathbf{A}
. One can see from the above equation that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N+1)}-\nu_{N+1}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}t.

(95)

We also have that for the
N\times N
top-left corner of
\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\right)^{{\dagger}}
:

\displaystyle\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}.

(96)

Therefore,
by
Itô’s formula we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}+\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}

\displaystyle\ \ +\mathrm{d}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}.

(97)

Using (
95
), we then obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\left(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N+1)}+\mathrm{d}\left(\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N+1)}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}

\displaystyle\ \ +\left(\frac{N+1}{2}-2\nu_{N+1}\right)\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}t.

(98)

Similarly to (
92
), by virtue of Levy’s characterization theorem, we can define the
N\times N
matrix-valued complex Brownian motion
\left(\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{t}^{(N)}\right)_{t\geq 0}
(different from (
92
)) by the formula

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{t}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{W}_{t}^{(N+1)}\times

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Using these we represent the SDE (
98
) in the following closed form, recalling (
96
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}}\left(\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{t}^{(N)}+\mathrm{d}\left(\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{t}^{(N)}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right)\times

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\sqrt{\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}}+\left(\frac{N+1}{2}-2\nu_{N+1}\right)\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{M}}_{t}^{(N)}&\mathbf{a}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}t.

(99)

Now, comparing the SDEs (
93
) and
(
99
)
and considering the definition of the parameter
\nu_{N}
, it follows that, both

\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}};t\geq 0\right)

and

\left(\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\right);t\geq 0\right)

satisfy the same equation, having a unique weak solution, and hence, these processes have the same distribution. This in particular implies
that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}s\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\Pi_{N}^{N+1}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\left(\mathbf{M}_{s}^{(\nu_{N+1})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}s\right),\ \ N\in\mathbb{N},

for any fixed
t>0
, as desired.
∎

We now give a direct proof for Theorem
3.9
for any initial condition.

Proof of Theorem
3.9
.

We will show

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{N+1}(t)\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}(\mathbf{x},\cdot)=\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)(\mathbf{x},\cdot),\ \ \forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1,+}^{\circ},\ \ \forall t\geq 0.

(100)

The case of general
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1,+}
then follows by the Feller property, see Proposition
3.5
. We have using the explicit expressions in (
78
) and (
36
), that (
100
) boils down to showing:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{W}_{N+1,+}}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{N+1}t}\det\left(\mathfrak{p}^{N+1}_{t}(x_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{z}\prec\mathbf{y}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}=\int_{\mathbb{W}_{N,+}}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{N}t}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{y}\prec\mathbf{x}}\det\left(\mathfrak{p}^{N}_{t}(y_{i},z_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}.

(101)

We write
\mathbf{x}\tilde{\prec}\mathbf{y}
to denote
y_{1}\geq x_{1}>y_{2}\geq x_{2}>\dots\geq x_{N}>y_{N+1}
, when
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
.
Then, we recall the well-known identity

\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}\tilde{\prec}\mathbf{y}}=\det\left(\varphi(x_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}
with

\displaystyle\varphi(x,y)=\begin{cases}-\mathbf{1}_{y>x},\ \ &x,y\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
1,\ \ &x=\textnormal{virt},\end{cases}

where we use the convention
x_{N+1}=\textnormal{virt}
for a “virtual variable”. Note that, we can replace
\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{y}\prec\mathbf{x}}
with
\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{y}\tilde{\prec}\mathbf{x}}
in (
101
).
Accordingly, with this replacement, the LHS of (
101
), by the Andreief identity, is equal to

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{N+1}t}\det\begin{pmatrix}[\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}\varphi^{*}](x_{1},z_{1})&[\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}\varphi^{*}](x_{1},z_{2})&\cdots&1\\
[\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}\varphi^{*}](x_{2},z_{1})&[\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}\varphi^{*}](x_{2},z_{2})&\cdots&1\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
[\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}(t)\varphi^{*}](x_{N+1},z_{1})&[\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}\varphi^{*}](x_{N+1},z_{2})&\cdots&1\end{pmatrix},

where
\varphi^{*}(x,y)=\varphi(y,x)
, and by definition
[\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}\varphi^{*}](x,y)
denotes compositition of kernels so that the entries of the matrix above are as follows:

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}\varphi^{*}](x_{i},z_{j})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}(x_{i},y)\varphi^{*}(y,z_{j})\mathrm{d}y=-\int_{z_{j}}^{\infty}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}(x_{i},y)\mathrm{d}y,\ \ \forall i,j=1,\dots,N+1.

For the RHS, we first expand the
\left(N+1\right)\times\left(N+1\right)
determinant corresponding to
\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}\tilde{\prec}\mathbf{y}}
. We next use the Andreief identity, and then again recombine terms to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{N+1}(-1)^{N+1-l}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{N}t}

\displaystyle\det\left([\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}](x_{i},z_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{N}t}\det\begin{pmatrix}[\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}](x_{1},z_{1})&[\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}](x_{1},z_{2})&\cdots&1\\
[\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}](x_{2},z_{1})&[\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}](x_{2},z_{2})&\cdots&1\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
[\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}](x_{N+1},z_{1})&[\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}](x_{N+1},z_{2})&\cdots&1\end{pmatrix},

(102)

where

\displaystyle[\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}](x_{i},z_{j})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi^{*}(x_{i},y)\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}(y,z_{j})\mathrm{d}y=-\int_{0}^{x_{i}}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}(y,z_{j})\mathrm{d}y,\ \ \forall i,j=1,\dots,N+1.

We now claim that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}(x,z)=\mathrm{e}^{\left(\frac{\eta}{2}+N\right)t}\partial_{x}\int_{z}^{\infty}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}(x,y)\mathrm{d}y\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}q_{t}(x,y).

(103)

We first note, the key algebraic consistency relation

\partial_{x}\mathsf{L}_{x}^{(N+1)}=\mathsf{L}_{x}^{(N)}\partial_{x}+(-\frac{\eta}{2}-N)\partial_{x}
.
Making use of this, it can be checked that
q_{t}(x,y)
is the fundamental solution to the Kolmogorov backward equation associated to the one-dimensional diffusion

\mathsf{L}_{x}^{(N)}
, which is unique, and hence, (
103
) holds.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{x_{i}}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}(y,z_{j})\mathrm{d}y=\mathrm{e}^{\left(\frac{\eta}{2}+N\right)t}\left[\int_{z_{j}}^{\infty}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}(0,y)\mathrm{d}y-\int_{z_{j}}^{\infty}\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N+1)}(x_{i},y)\mathrm{d}y\right],\ \forall i,j=1,\dots,N+1.

(104)

One can now substitute this in the determinant in (
102
) and use column operations to show the desired identity, by noting that
\lambda_{N+1}-\lambda_{N}=N(-\frac{\eta}{2}-N)
.
∎

3.3

Invariant measure and convergence to equilibrium

The inverse Laguerre ensemble
\mathfrak{M}_{N}^{\eta}

of parameter
\eta>-1
is the probability measure on
\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{N}^{\eta}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{(\eta)}}\Delta_{N}^{2}(\textbf{x})\prod_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{-\eta-2N}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{x_{i}}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},

(105)

for some normalisation constant
\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{(\eta)}
. We now show that the solution
(\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0)
of (
4
) started from arbitrary initial condition, converges to
\mathfrak{M}_{N}^{\eta}
.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
\eta>-1
and consider the unique strong solution
(\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0)
of (
4
). Suppose
\mathfrak{K}\in\mathscr{M}_{\textnormal{p}}(\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
and that
\mathsf{Law}(\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(0))=\mathfrak{K}
. Then, as
t\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t)\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{Z},\ \ \textnormal{where }\mathsf{Law}(\mathbf{Z})=\mathfrak{M}_{N}^{\eta}.

(106)

In particular,
\mathfrak{M}_{N}^{\eta}
is the unique invariant measure of
(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.

Proof.

We use the corresponding matrix process to prove the result.
Recall that from (
89
), the unique strong solution
(\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)};t\geq 0)

starting from
\mathbf{H}_{0}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
can be written as

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{H}^{(N)}_{0}\left(\mathbf{M}_{t}^{(-\nu_{N})}\right)^{-{\dagger}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{N}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{N}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}u.

(107)

Let us take
\mathbf{H}_{0}^{(N)}
such that
\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N}\left(\mathbf{H}_{0}^{(N)}\right)\right)=\mathfrak{K}
(and independent of the driving Brownian motions). Then, from Lemma 2.2 in
[
6
]
the first term converges to
0
almost surely as
t\to\infty
. Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{t}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{N}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{N}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}u,\ \ \text{as }t\to\infty.

(108)

Now, by the matrix Dufresne identity proven in
[
96
]
, see also
[
74
]
, we know that

\mathsf{Law}\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{N}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})}\left(\mathbf{N}_{u}^{(\nu_{N})}\right)^{{\dagger}}\mathrm{d}u\right)=\mathfrak{IL}_{N}^{\eta},

where
\mathfrak{IL}_{N}^{\eta}
is the inverse Laguerre measure on
\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
given by the formula,

\mathfrak{IL}_{N}^{\eta}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{A})=\det\left(\mathbf{A}\right)^{-\eta-2N}\exp\left(-\textnormal{Tr}\left(\mathbf{A}^{-1}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{A},

with
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{A}
denoting the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{H}_{+}(N)
, for some normalisation constant
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{N}^{(\eta)}
.
By Weyl’s integration formula we know that

\left(\mathsf{eval}_{N}\right)_{*}\mathfrak{IL}_{N}^{\eta}=\mathfrak{M}_{N}^{\eta},

and this concludes the proof of (
106
). The final statement follows by a standard argument.
∎

As a consequence of Theorem
3.9
and uniqueness of the invariant measures from Proposition
3.14
we obtain the consistency relation between the inverse Laguerre ensembles; see also
[
7
]
for a direct proof, using orthogonal polynomials.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
\eta>-1
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}_{N+1}\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}=\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}_{N},\ \ \forall N\in\mathbb{N}.

(109)

We also have the following equivalent abstract description of the measure
\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}
from the introduction.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
\eta>-1
. The unique
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathscr{M}_{\textnormal{p}}(\Omega_{+})
satisfying:

\mathfrak{m}\Lambda_{N}^{\infty}=\mathfrak{M}_{N}^{\eta},\ \ \forall N\in\mathbb{N},

is given by
\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}
.

Proof.

This is the main result of
[
7
]
.
∎

3.4

Proof of Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.7

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

This follows by taking the semigroups
\left(\mathsf{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
to be
\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
in Theorem
2.6
, Theorem
2.31
and Proposition
2.33
all of whose conditions are satisfied by virtue of Proposition
3.5
and Proposition
3.7
and Theorem
3.9
and Theorem
2.22
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

By taking
\left(\mathsf{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
to be
\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
this is a consequence of Theorem
2.10
by virtue of Proposition
3.14
and Proposition
3.16
.
∎

3.5

On the Gibbs property

We briefly discuss a new Gibbs resampling property, see
[
25
]
, underlying our model. We assume the reader is familiar with the basic terminology of Gibbsian line ensembles, see
[
25
]
. This subsection is not part of our argument and can be skipped.

For
\eta>-1
, there exists an explicit spectral expansion of
\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}(x,y)
in terms of special functions, see
[
114
]
. This expansion is well-adapted to looking at the
t\to\infty
asymptotics of
\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{(N)}(x,y)
and using it one can prove that the semigroup
\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is exactly the transition semigroup of
N
independent one-dimensional diffusions with generator
\mathsf{L}^{(N)}
from (
77
) conditioned to never intersect
20
20

20

We note that this statement does not follow directly from the Doob
h
-transform structure of
\left(\mathfrak{P}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
in (
78
). One needs to compute the asymptotics of the first collision time of the
N
independent one-dimensional diffusions
\mathsf{L}^{(N)}
in order show that these two structures coincide and this is in general a delicate problem.
. In particular,
(\mathfrak{x}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0)
, viewed as a line ensemble with
N
lines, see
[
25
]
, enjoys a certain Gibbs resampling property with respect to the law of conditioned
\mathsf{L}^{(N)}
-bridges, see
[
35
]
for the rigorous definition of diffusion bridges; in the framework of
[
25
]
we replace Brownian bridges with
\mathsf{L}^{(N)}
-bridges. Similarly, the rescaled process
(\mathsf{x}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0)
from (
10
) enjoys a Gibbs resampling property with respect to
\mathsf{L}_{\textnormal{rsc}}^{(N)}
-bridges, where
\mathsf{L}_{\textnormal{rsc}}^{(N)}
is the generator of the rescaled by
N^{-1}
diffusion
\mathsf{L}^{(N)}
given by:

\mathsf{L}_{\textnormal{rsc}}^{(N)}=\frac{x^{2}}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}+\left(\left(1-\frac{\eta}{2}-N\right)x+\frac{1}{2N}\right)\partial_{x},

with transition density denoted by
\mathfrak{q}_{t}^{(N)}(x,y)
. It is then a natural question whether, the limiting paths
((\mathsf{x}_{i}(t))_{i=1}^{\infty};t\geq 0)
, which we do not yet know if they are non-intersecting, enjoy a Gibbs resampling property as well. If they did this would give an alternative, to the one in Section
4.1
below, albeit more complicated, way to prove non-intersection.

One can show, that given the convergence of the paths, that we already know from Theorem
1.3
, this question really boils down, modulo assumptions of lesser importance to avoid pathological situations, to convergence of the free
\mathsf{L}_{\textnormal{rsc}}^{(N)}
-bridge, whose time-inhomogeneous generator, in case of the bridge ending at point
y
at time
T>0
, is given by, for
0\leq t<T
, see
[
35
]
,

\frac{x^{2}}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}+\left(\left(1-\frac{\eta}{2}-N\right)x+\frac{1}{2N}+x^{2}\partial_{x}\log\mathfrak{q}_{T-t}^{(N)}(x,y)\right)\partial_{x},

to a limiting diffusion bridge as
N\to\infty
. This question turns out to be complicated. The spectral expansion in
[
114
]
is not well-adapted for looking at the
N\to\infty
limit of the above expression. Nevertheless, using some judicious transformations, it is possible to connect this problem to a problem of asymptotics of the logarithmic derivative of the transition kernel of a Schrödinger semigroup with an explicit
N
-dependent Morse potential
\mathsf{V}_{N}(x)
, see
[
68
,
69
]
. What we gain by doing this is that all
N
-dependence of the problem is now in the potential
\mathsf{V}_{N}(x)
and it is easy to see that it converges to an explicit potential
\mathsf{V}_{\infty}(x)
. Alas, the limiting potential
\mathsf{V}_{\infty}(x)
has the “wrong” sign and the associated Schrödinger semigroup blows up! In particular, the transition kernel of the Schrödinger semigroup with potential
\mathsf{V}_{N}(x)
diverges as
N\to\infty
. However, its logarithmic derivative may still well converge, due a subtle cancellation, and so this does not contradict the existence of a limiting Gibbs property.

The naive guess is that we have an exponential Brownian Gibbs property in the limit. The simple intuition comes from the fact that if the vanishing
1/2N
drift term in
\mathsf{L}_{\textnormal{rsc}}^{(N)}
was absent then
\mathsf{L}_{\textnormal{rsc}}^{(N)}
would have been an exponential Brownian motion with
N
-dependent drift and the corresponding bridge would have been an exponential Brownian bridge identically for all
N
.

4

The ISDE

In this section we first prove that
\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty}
almost surely consists of non-intersecting paths in Theorem
4.3
and then in Theorem
4.5
that it solves the ISDE (
13
). The main idea will be to use various “characteristic polynomial”-like objects associated to the finite-dimensional dynamics
(\mathsf{x}^{(N)}_{i}(\cdot))_{i=1}^{N}
, first as Lyapunov functions, to control uniformly in
N
, the times two paths come close and then to show convergence of the singular drift term. We need some preliminaries.

We recall a result from
[
9
]
on convergence of polynomials with real zeros to analytic functions that we use as one of the ingredients in the proofs of
Theorem
4.3
and Theorem
4.5
below. Given,
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
define the polynomial

\displaystyle\mathfrak{U}_{N}\left(z;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(1-x_{j}^{(N)}z\right).

This is the reverse characteristic polynomial of a matrix with eigenvalues
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}
. We also have the following definition.

Definition 4.1
.

Define the entire functions
\mathsf{E}(z;\omega)
and
\mathsf{E}_{+}(z;\omega)
, indexed by
\omega\in\Omega
and
\omega\in\Omega_{+}
respectively,
given by their Hadamard products:

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}(z;\omega)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma z-\frac{\delta-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left[(x_{i}^{+})^{2}+(x_{i}^{-})^{2}\right]}{2}z^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{x_{j}^{+}z}\left(1-x_{j}^{+}z\right)\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-x_{j}^{-}z}\left(1+x_{j}^{-}z\right),\ \ \omega\in\Omega,

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{+}(z;\omega)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma z}\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{x_{j}z}\left(1-x_{j}z\right),\ \ \omega\in\Omega_{+}.

Then, we have the following result, see
Proposition 2.5 and Remark 2.7 of
[
9
]
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Suppose
\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}\in\left(\mathbb{W}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
such that
\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\omega\in\Omega
. Then, the following convergence holds uniformly on compact sets in
\mathbb{C}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{U}_{N}\left(z;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\to\mathsf{E}(z;\omega),\ \ \textnormal{ as }\;N\to\infty.

If instead
\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}\in\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
such that
\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\omega\in\Omega_{+}
, then the following convergence holds uniformly on compact sets in
\mathbb{C}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{U}_{N}\left(z;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\to\mathsf{E}_{+}(z;\omega),\ \ \text{ as }\;N\to\infty.

(110)

Note that this convergence statement is consistent with the embedding (
39
) of
\Omega_{+}
into
\Omega
. Below we will only use the result for
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
and
\Omega_{+}
.

4.1

Non-intersection via characteristic polynomials

We use the following notation convention throughout. For
(\mathbf{x},\gamma)\in\Omega_{+}
we write
\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}
for the law of
\left(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\Omega_{+}};t\geq 0\right)
from Theorem
1.3
starting from
(\mathbf{x},\gamma)
and abusing notation (as we do not assume that all these processes are defined on the same probability space unless otherwise stated), for
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
, we write
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}}
for the law of the process solving (
10
) starting from
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}
. We write
\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}}
for the corresponding expectations.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{\infty,+}^{\circ}
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, with
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}x_{i}\leq\gamma
. Consider the Feller process on
\Omega_{+}
,
\left(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\Omega_{+}};t\geq 0\right)=\left(\left(\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}(t)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty},\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t)\right);t\geq 0\right)
from Theorem
1.3
with initial condition
\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\Omega_{+}}=(\mathbf{x},\gamma)
. Then, for all
T\geq 0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)>0\right)

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{n+1}(t)\right|>0\right)

\displaystyle=1.

Proof.

We prove the result using an inductive argument. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{\circ}
converge to
(\mathbf{x},\gamma)\in\Omega_{+}
under the embedding (
41
).
Let
\mathsf{x}^{(N)}
be the solution to the equation (
10
) started at
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}
.
We will make use of the
\log
transform of (
10
), which we consider in its integral form:

\displaystyle\log\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)

\displaystyle=\log x_{i}^{(N)}+\mathsf{w}_{i}(t)-\frac{1+\eta}{2}t+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2N\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\ \ +\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\frac{\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}{\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}s,\ \ i=1,\dots,N.

(111)

Let us continue with some definitions. We always use the convention
\inf\emptyset=\infty
in what follows.
Define,
for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the following stopping times (with respect to the natural filtration) associated to
\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}\right)_{i=1}^{N}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}^{N}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\inf\left\{t\geq 0;\mathsf{x}_{n}^{(N)}(t)\leq\epsilon\right\},\ \ \epsilon>0,\ \ n=1,\dots,N

\displaystyle\tau_{n}^{N}(\delta)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\inf\left\{t\geq 0;\left|1-\frac{\mathsf{x}_{n+1}^{(N)}(t)}{\mathsf{x}_{n}^{(N)}(t)}\right|\leq\delta\right\},\ \ \delta>0,\ \ n=1,\dots,N-1,

\displaystyle\theta_{n}^{N}(r)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\inf\left\{t\geq 0;\mathsf{x}_{n}^{(N)}(t)\geq r\right\},\ \ r>0,\ \ n=1,\dots,N,

and
analogously, the stopping times (with respect to the natural filtration) associated to
\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{\infty}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\inf\left\{t\geq 0;\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)\leq\epsilon\right\},\ \ \epsilon>0,\ \ n\in\mathbb{N},

\displaystyle\tau_{n}(\delta)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\inf\left\{t\geq 0;\left|1-\frac{\mathsf{x}_{n+1}(t)}{\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)}\right|\leq\delta\right\},\ \ \delta>0,\ \ n\in\mathbb{N},

\displaystyle\theta_{n}(r)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\inf\left\{t\geq 0;\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)\geq r\right\},\ \ r>0,\ \ n\in\mathbb{N}.

Note that, by Lemma
3.1
,
\tau_{n}^{N}(\delta)
is well-defined for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\leq N-1
.
It will be justified later on that
\tau_{n}(\delta)
is also well-defined for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Now, let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. We define for any
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{\circ}
, the following variant of the characteristic polynomial associated to
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}
,

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=n+1}^{N}\left(1-\frac{x_{j}^{(N)}}{x_{i}^{(N)}}\right),\ \ n=1,\dots,N-1,

and consider the Lyapunov function
given as:

\displaystyle f_{n}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}-\log\left(\mathrm{\Psi}_{n}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right),\ \ n=1,\dots,N-1.

(112)

It is easy to see that
f_{n}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)>0
for all
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{\circ}
and
n=1,\dots,N-1
.
We next define

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}^{N}(R)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\inf\left\{t\geq 0;f_{n}^{N}\left(\mathsf{x}^{(N)}(t)\right)\geq R\right\},\ \ R>0,\ \ n=1,\dots,N-1.

Observe that,

\displaystyle\left\{\tau_{n}^{N}(\delta)\leq a\right\}\subset\left\{\kappa_{n}^{N}\left(\log(\delta^{-1})\right)\leq a\right\},\ \ \textnormal{for }\delta<1.

(113)

We are going to prove by induction on
n
that
for all
T\geq 0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)>0\right)

\displaystyle=1,

(114)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\left|1-\frac{\mathsf{x}_{n+1}(t)}{\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)}\right|>0\right)

\displaystyle=1.

(115)

Note that, this implies non-collision, since given (
114
) and (
115
), we have almost surely,

\displaystyle\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{n+1}(t)\right|\geq\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\left|1-\frac{\mathsf{x}_{n+1}(t)}{\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)}\right|,

and hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{n+1}(t)\right|>0\right)=1.

The base case.

Using (
111
), we have for any
t\geq 0
that

\displaystyle\log\mathsf{x}_{1}^{(N)}\left(t\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)\wedge\theta_{1}^{N}(r)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\log x_{1}^{(N)}-\left|\frac{1+\eta}{2}\right|t+\mathsf{w}_{1}\left(t\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)\wedge\theta_{1}^{N}(r)\right).

Therefore, since
t\mapsto\mathsf{w}_{1}\left(t\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)\wedge\theta_{1}^{N}(r)\right)
is a mean-zero martingale, taking expectation of both sides we obtain

\displaystyle\log r+\log\epsilon\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{x}^{(N)}}\left(\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)<t\wedge\theta_{1}^{N}(r)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\log x_{1}^{(N)}-\left|\frac{1+\eta}{2}\right|t,

and thus, for any
\epsilon<1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}}\left(\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)<t\wedge\theta_{1}^{N}(r)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\log r-\log x_{1}^{(N)}+\left|\frac{1+\eta}{2}\right|t}{|\log\epsilon|}.

(116)

Observe that, the bound in (
116
) converges as
N\to\infty
(recall that
x_{1}^{(N)}\to x_{1}>0
). We would like to conclude that the same bound (with
x_{1}
in the formula instead) holds for the stopping times
\sigma_{1}(\epsilon)
and
\theta_{1}(r)
corresponding to the limit paths. A little argument is needed though. These stopping times, viewed as functionals on paths are not continuous, but fortunately they are not far from continuous. For more details see Chapter VI, Sections 2,3 of
[
47
]
which we follow closely. For
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
f\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we define

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{\geq}^{a}(f)=\inf\left\{t\geq 0;f(t)\geq a\right\},\ \ \mathsf{S}_{\leq}^{a}(f)=\inf\left\{t\geq 0;f(t)\leq a\right\}.

Write
\mathbb{R}_{+,\textnormal{ext}}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
. For fixed
f\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
define the map
\mathsf{B}_{f}
by,

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{f}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+,\textnormal{ext}}^{2}

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\mathsf{S}_{\leq}^{a}(f),\mathsf{S}_{\geq}^{b}(f)\right),

which from Lemma 2.10 of Chapter VI of
[
47
]
only has jump discontinuities, while for fixed
(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
define the map
\mathsf{A}_{(a,b)}
by,

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}_{(a,b)}:C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+,\textnormal{ext}}^{2}

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\mathsf{S}_{\leq}^{a}(f),\mathsf{S}_{\geq}^{b}(f)\right).

Now, if we let
\mathfrak{D}_{(a,b)}
denote the set of discontinuities of the map
\mathsf{A}_{(a,b)}
and
\mathsf{D}(f)
denote the set of discontinuities of the map
\mathsf{B}_{f}
, then from Proposition 2.11 of Chapter VI of
[
47
]
, since
\mathsf{A}_{(a,b)}
is continuous at every
f
so that
(a,b)\notin\mathsf{D}(f)
, we obtain that,

\mathfrak{D}_{(a,b)}\subset\left\{f\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+}):(a,b)\in\mathsf{D}(f)\right\}.

Let us write
\mathbb{P}^{(1)}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}
for
\mathsf{Law}(\mathsf{x}_{1})
on
C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
under
\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}
. Then, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}=\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}:\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}^{(1)}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{(a,b)}\right)>0\right\}\subset\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}:\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}^{(1)}\left((a,b)\in\mathsf{D}(\mathsf{x}_{1})\right)>0\right\}.

Then, by virtue of the fact that the map
\mathsf{B}_{f}
only has jump discontinuities, we obtain, see Lemma 3.12 of Chapter VI of
[
47
]
, that the sets
\mathcal{V}_{1}=\{a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:(a,b)\in\mathcal{V}\}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}=\{b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:(a,b)\in\mathcal{V}\}
are at most countable. In particular, we can find sequences
(\epsilon_{m})_{m=1}^{\infty}
and
(r_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{\infty}
, with
\epsilon_{m}\to 0
and
r_{\ell}\to\infty
, as
m,\ell\to\infty
, so that for any
m,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}^{(1)}(\mathfrak{D}_{(\epsilon_{m},r_{\ell})})=0
. Now, observe that,

\left(\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon),\theta_{1}^{N}(r)\right)=\mathsf{A}_{(\epsilon,r)}\left(\mathsf{x}_{1}^{(N)}\right),\ \ \left(\sigma_{1}(\epsilon),\theta_{1}(r)\right)=\mathsf{A}_{(\epsilon,r)}(\mathsf{x}_{1}).

Hence, by the continuous mapping theorem, we obtain that for all
m,l\in\mathbb{N}
, as
N\to\infty
,

\left(\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon_{m}),\theta_{1}^{N}(r_{\ell})\right)\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\longrightarrow}\left(\sigma_{1}(\epsilon_{m}),\theta_{1}(r_{\ell})\right).

By the Portmanteau theorem for convergence in distribution, we get that, for any open set
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\left(\sigma_{1}(\epsilon_{m}),\theta_{1}(r_{\ell})\right)\in\mathcal{O}\right)\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}}\left(\left(\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon_{m}),\theta_{1}^{N}(r_{\ell})\right)\in\mathcal{O}\right).

From this and (
116
) we conclude that (for
\epsilon_{m}<1
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\sigma_{1}(\epsilon_{m})<t\wedge\theta_{1}(r_{\ell})\right)\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{x}^{(N)}}\left(\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon_{m})<t\wedge\theta_{1}^{N}(r_{\ell})\right)\leq\frac{\log r_{\ell}-\log x_{1}+\left|\frac{1+\eta}{2}\right|t}{|\log\epsilon_{m}|},

and hence, by the monotone convergence theorem,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\lim_{m\to\infty}\sigma_{1}(\epsilon_{m})<t\wedge\theta_{1}(r_{\ell})\right)=0.

Therefore, by taking
\ell,t\to\infty
, it follows, by virtue of the fact that (recall that
r_{\ell}\to\infty
),
\theta_{1}(r_{\ell})\overset{\textnormal{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\infty
, that
\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\lim_{m\to\infty}\sigma_{1}(\epsilon_{m})=\infty\right)=1
, namely, since
\epsilon_{m}\to 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathsf{x}_{1}(t)>0\right)=1.

(117)

We now proceed to prove the second assertion.
Using Itô’s formula, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}f_{1}^{N}\left(\mathsf{x}^{(N)}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\mathsf{M}_{1}^{(N)}(t)+\frac{N-1}{2N\mathsf{x}_{1}^{(N)}(t)}\mathrm{d}t-\sum_{j=2}^{N}\frac{\left(\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(t)\right)^{2}}{\left(\mathsf{x}_{1}^{(N)}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(t)\right)^{2}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\ \ +\sum_{j=2}^{N}\sum_{\ell=2,\ell\neq j}^{N}\frac{\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(t)\mathsf{x}_{\ell}^{(N)}(t)}{\left(\mathsf{x}_{1}^{(N)}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{\ell}^{(N)}(t)\right)\left(\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{\ell}^{(N)}(t)\right)}\mathrm{d}t.

(118)

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{1}^{(N)}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}(N-1)\mathsf{w}_{1}(t)+\sum_{\ell=2}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathsf{x}_{1}^{(N)}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{w}_{1}(s)-\mathsf{x}_{\ell}^{(N)}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{w}_{\ell}(s)}{\mathsf{x}_{1}^{(N)}-\mathsf{x}_{\ell}^{(N)}},\ \ \forall t\geq 0,

is a local martingale. Now, we observe, and this is key, that for any
t\geq 0
, the last term on the RHS of (
118
) is non-positive, by virtue of the elementary inequality, for
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{\circ}
,

\sum_{j=2}^{N}\sum_{\ell=2,\ell\neq j}^{N}\frac{y_{j}y_{\ell}}{(y_{1}-y_{\ell})(y_{j}-y_{\ell})}=\sum_{\underset{j<\ell}{j,\ell=2}}^{N}\frac{y_{j}y_{\ell}}{y_{j}-y_{\ell}}\left(\frac{1}{y_{1}-y_{\ell}}-\frac{1}{y_{1}-y_{j}}\right)\leq 0.

Thus, we can drop this term and write

\displaystyle f_{1}^{N}\left(\mathsf{x}^{(N)}\left(t\wedge\kappa_{1}^{N}(R)\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq f_{1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)+\mathsf{M}_{1}^{(N)}\left(t\wedge\kappa_{1}^{N}(R)\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\ \ +\int_{0}^{t\wedge\kappa_{1}^{N}(R)\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)}\frac{1}{2\mathsf{x}_{1}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq f_{1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)+\mathsf{M}_{1}^{(N)}\left(t\wedge\kappa_{1}^{N}(R)\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)\right)+\frac{t}{\epsilon}.

Now, since the stopped process
t\mapsto\mathsf{M}_{1}^{(N)}\left(t\wedge\kappa_{1}^{N}(R)\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)\right)
is a mean-zero martingale, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}}\left[f_{1}^{N}\left(\mathsf{x}^{(N)}\left(t\wedge\kappa_{1}^{N}(R)\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq f_{1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)+\frac{t}{\epsilon}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle R\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}}\left(\kappa_{1}^{N}(R)<t\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)\right)\leq\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}f_{1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)+\frac{t}{\epsilon}.

Note that, since

\omega\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}(\mathbf{x},\gamma)
, it follows from Proposition
4.2
that

\Psi_{1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)
converges to a strictly positive limit,
and hence,

C\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}f_{1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)<\infty
. We thus have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}}\left(\kappa_{1}^{N}(R)<t\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)\right)\leq\frac{C+\frac{t}{\epsilon}}{R}.

Let
\delta<1
be arbitrary. We have in view of (
113
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}}\left(\tau_{1}^{N}(\delta)<t\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon)\right)\leq\frac{C+\frac{t}{\epsilon}}{|\log\delta|},

(119)

and note that this bound is independent of
N
. Observe that,

\tau_{1}^{N}(\delta)=\mathsf{S}_{\leq}^{\delta}\left(\left|1-\frac{\mathsf{x}_{2}^{(N)}}{\mathsf{x}_{1}^{(N)}}\right|\right),\ \ \tau_{1}(\delta)=\mathsf{S}_{\leq}^{\delta}\left(\left|1-\frac{\mathsf{x}_{2}}{\mathsf{x}_{1}}\right|\right),

and that by virtue of (
117
),
t\mapsto|\mathsf{x}_{1}(t)|^{-1}|\mathsf{x}_{1}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{2}(t)|
is almost surely, a well-defined continuous function. We now argue similarly to before to obtain the existence of sequences, abusing notation (the sequence
(\epsilon_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{\infty}
here may have nothing to do with the sequence
(\epsilon_{m})_{m=1}^{\infty}
from earlier),
(\delta_{m})_{m=1}^{\infty}
,
(\epsilon_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{\infty}
, with
\delta_{m},\epsilon_{\ell}\to 0
, as
m,\ell\to\infty
, so that, for all
m,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, as
N\to\infty
,

\left(\tau_{1}^{N}(\delta_{m}),\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon_{\ell})\right)\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\longrightarrow}\left(\tau_{1}(\delta_{m}),\sigma_{1}(\epsilon_{\ell})\right).

Hence, by taking
N\to\infty
in (
119
), one obtains, for
\delta_{m}<1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\tau_{1}(\delta_{m})<t\wedge\sigma_{1}(\epsilon_{\ell})\right)\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}}\left(\tau_{1}^{N}(\delta_{m})<t\wedge\sigma_{1}^{N}(\epsilon_{\ell})\right)\leq\frac{C+\frac{t}{\epsilon_{\ell}}}{|\log\delta_{m}|}.

It thus follows that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\lim_{m\to\infty}\tau_{1}(\delta_{m})<t\wedge\sigma_{1}(\epsilon_{\ell})\right)=0.

Now, we take
t\to\infty
,
and then
\ell\to\infty
, to finally get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\lim_{m\to\infty}\tau_{1}(\delta_{m})\geq\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\sigma_{1}(\epsilon_{\ell})\right)=1.

But
\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\sigma_{1}(\epsilon_{\ell})=\infty\right)=1
by virtue of (
117
).
This implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\lim_{m\to\infty}\tau_{1}(\delta_{m})=\infty\right)=1.

That is, for all
T\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\left|1-\frac{\mathsf{x}_{2}(t)}{\mathsf{x}_{1}(t)}\right|>0\right)=1,

(120)

which completes the proof of the case
n=1
.

The inductive step.
We next assume that (
114
) and (
115
) hold for
n=k
, and prove them for
n=k+1
.
From (
111
), we can write

\displaystyle\log\mathsf{x}_{k+1}^{(N)}\left(t\wedge\sigma_{k+1}^{N}(\epsilon)\wedge\tau_{k}^{N}(\delta)\wedge\theta_{k+1}^{N}(r)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\log x_{k+1}^{(N)}+\mathsf{w}_{k+1}\left(t\wedge\sigma_{k+1}^{N}(\epsilon)\wedge\tau_{k}^{N}(\delta)\wedge\theta_{k+1}^{N}(r)\right)

\displaystyle-\left|\frac{1+\eta}{2}\right|

\displaystyle t+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\sigma_{k+1}^{N}(\epsilon)\wedge\tau_{k}^{N}(\delta)\wedge\theta_{k+1}^{N}(r)}\sum_{j=1,j\neq k+1}^{k}\frac{\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(t)}{\mathsf{x}_{k+1}^{(N)}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(t)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\log x_{k+1}^{(N)}

\displaystyle-\left|\frac{1+\eta}{2}\right|t+\mathsf{w}_{k+1}\left(t\wedge\sigma_{k+1}^{N}(\epsilon)\wedge\tau_{k}^{N}(\delta)\wedge\theta_{k+1}^{N}(r)\right)-\frac{kt}{\delta},

and thus, we have,

\displaystyle\log r+\log\epsilon\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{x}^{(N)}}\left(\sigma_{k+1}^{N}(\epsilon)<t\wedge\tau_{k}^{N}(\delta)\wedge\theta_{k+1}^{N}(r)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\log x_{k+1}^{(N)}-\left|\frac{1+\eta}{2}\right|t-\frac{kt}{\delta}.

One can check, by making use of the induction hypothesis, that as previously, there exist sequences
(\epsilon_{m})_{m=1}^{\infty}
,
(\delta_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{\infty}
,
(r_{q})_{q=1}^{\infty}
, with
\epsilon_{m},\delta_{\ell}\to 0
,
r_{q}\to\infty
, as
m,\ell,q\to\infty
, so that the corresponding stopping times converge in distribution, as
N\to\infty
, to their limiting counterparts, and in particular we obtain,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\lim_{m\to\infty}\sigma_{k+1}(\epsilon_{m})<t\wedge\tau_{k}(\delta_{\ell})\wedge\theta_{k+1}(r_{q})\right)=0.

Hence, it follows that
\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\lim_{m\to\infty}\sigma_{k+1}(\epsilon_{m})=\infty\right)=1
, if we take
m\to\infty
first, and then,
t,\ell,q\to\infty
whereby
\tau_{k}(\delta_{\ell}),\theta_{k+1}(r_{q})\overset{\textnormal{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\infty
. Equivalently,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\mathsf{x}_{k+1}(t)>0\right)=1.

We next apply Itô’s formula to
f_{k+1}^{N}
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}f_{k+1}^{N}\left(\mathsf{x}^{(N)}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\mathsf{M}_{k+1}^{(N)}(t)+\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\frac{N-k-1}{2N\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)}\mathrm{d}t-\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k+2}^{N}\frac{\left(\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(t)\right)^{2}}{\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(t)\right)^{2}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\ \ +\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k+2}^{N}\sum_{\underset{\ell\neq i,j}{\ell=1}}^{N}\frac{\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(t)\mathsf{x}_{\ell}^{(N)}(t)}{\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{\ell}^{(N)}(t)\right)\left(\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{\ell}^{(N)}(t)\right)}\mathrm{d}t,

where
t\mapsto\mathsf{M}_{k+1}^{(N)}(t)
stands for the corresponding local martingale term.
We note, and this is again important, that the last term in the above expression is non-positive for any
t\geq 0
, by virtue of the inequality, for
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{\circ}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k+2}^{N}\sum_{\underset{\ell\neq i,j}{\ell=1}}^{N}\frac{y_{j}y_{\ell}}{(y_{i}-y_{\ell})(y_{j}-y_{\ell})}\leq 0,

which is easily seen to be true since the LHS can be written as,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k+2}^{N}\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq i}^{k+1}\frac{y_{j}y_{\ell}}{(y_{i}-y_{\ell})(y_{j}-y_{\ell})}+\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\sum_{j=k+2}^{N}\sum_{\underset{\ell\neq j}{\ell=k+2}}^{N}\frac{y_{j}y_{\ell}}{(y_{i}-y_{\ell})(y_{j}-y_{\ell})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=k+2}^{N}\sum_{\underset{i<\ell}{i,\ell=1}}^{k+1}\frac{y_{j}}{y_{i}-y_{\ell}}\left(\frac{y_{\ell}}{y_{j}-y_{\ell}}-\frac{y_{i}}{y_{j}-y_{i}}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\sum_{\underset{j<\ell}{j,\ell=k+2}}^{N}\frac{y_{j}y_{\ell}}{y_{j}-y_{\ell}}\left(\frac{1}{y_{i}-y_{\ell}}-\frac{1}{y_{i}-y_{j}}\right)\leq 0.

Thus, by dropping it, we have

\displaystyle f_{k+1}^{N}\left(\mathsf{x}^{(N)}\left(t\wedge\kappa_{k+1}^{N}(R)\wedge\sigma_{k+1}^{N}(\epsilon)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq f_{k+1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)+\mathsf{M}_{k+1}^{(N)}\left(t\wedge\kappa_{k+1}^{N}(R)\wedge\sigma_{k+1}^{N}(\epsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\ \ +\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\kappa_{k+1}^{N}(R)\wedge\sigma_{k+1}^{N}(\epsilon)}\frac{1}{2\mathsf{x}_{n}^{(N)}(t)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq f_{k+1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)+\mathsf{M}_{k+1}^{(N)}\left(t\wedge\kappa_{k+1}^{N}(R)\wedge\sigma_{k+1}^{N}(\epsilon)\right)+\frac{k}{\epsilon}t.

One can now follow the argument for the case
n=1
to establish the desired result in the exact same way.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

We note that in the argument above we only needed to take as input convergence of the paths and not necessarily convergence in
C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\Omega_{+})
. Had we not even known convergence of the paths, the argument would in any case give that any possible subsequential limits would need to consist of non-intersecting paths.

4.2

The ISDE via characteristic polynomials

We now prove that
\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty}
solves the ISDE (
13
). Beyond the use
21
21

21

We also present a slightly shorter argument (directly using convergence on
\Omega_{+}
and non-intersection of the paths) for convergence of the singular drift term in Remark
4.7
that does not involve the use of characteristic polynomials. Nevertheless, these two arguments are basically equivalent, since convergence on
\Omega_{+}
implies convergence of the corresponding polynomials.
of characteristic polynomials to show convergence of the singular drift, it is interesting to note the following. In the construction of weak solutions to finite-dimensional SDE, see
[
103
]
, the driving Brownian motions can usually
22
22

22

The upshot is that one wants to construct the driving Brownian motions from the solution, see
[
103
]
. If the noise degenerates, one needs an auxiliary, independent from the solution, Brownian motion, see
[
103
]
. However, in our setting the noise does not degenerate as
\mathsf{x}_{i}>0
and this is not the source of the additional randomness.
be taken to be measurable with respect to the natural filtration generated by the solution. Here, the driving Brownian motions
(\mathsf{w}_{i}(\cdot))_{i=1}^{\infty}
will in fact be measurable not with the filtration generated by
\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty}
on its own but rather with the filtration generated by the enhanced process
\mathbf{X}_{\cdot}^{\Omega_{+}}=\left((\mathsf{x}_{i}(\cdot))_{i=1}^{\infty},\boldsymbol{\gamma}(\cdot)\right)
. This is another illustration of the phenomenon that in the infinite-dimensional limit additional information is created.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{\infty,+}^{\circ}
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}x_{i}\leq\gamma
.
Let
\mathbf{X}_{\cdot}^{\Omega_{+}}
and
\mathsf{x}(\cdot)=\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty}
be as in Theorem
4.3
. Then,
\mathsf{x}(\cdot)
is a weak solution, starting from
\mathbf{x}
, to the ISDE (
13
). Moreover, we have that,

\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left(\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}_{t}\in\Omega_{+}^{0},\;\forall t>0\right)=1,\ \ \forall(\mathbf{x},\gamma)\in\Omega_{+}.

(121)

Proof of Theorem
4.5
.

We first prove that there exists a filtered probability space, on which independent standard Brownian motions
(\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
adapted to the filtration and an equal in law copy
(\tilde{\mathsf{x}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
of
(\mathsf{x},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
, also adapted to the filtration, are defined, such that almost surely, for all
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)=x_{i}+\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(s)+\int_{0}^{t}\left(-\frac{\eta}{2}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)+\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{\infty}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}\right)\mathrm{d}s,\ \ i\in\mathbb{N}.

(122)

The filtration will be the natural filtration of
(\tilde{\mathsf{x}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
. Recall that the natural filtration
(\mathscr{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of a process
(\mathfrak{R}_{t};t\geq 0)
is given by, for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathscr{F}_{t}=\sigma\left(\mathfrak{R}_{t};0\leq s\leq t\right),

where for a collection of random variables
\mathcal{C}
, we write
\sigma(\mathcal{C})
for the sigma algebra generated by them.

We now prove this claim. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+}^{\circ}
be such that it converges, under the embedding (
41
), to
(\mathbf{x},\gamma)
. Let
\mathsf{x}^{(N)}
be the solution to the equation (
10
) starting at
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}
.
Writing the equation in the integral form, one has for
i=1,2,\dots,N
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(t)=x_{i}^{(N)}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\mathrm{d}\mathsf{w}_{i}(s)-\frac{\eta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{2N}+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\frac{\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}{\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\mathsf{x}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}s.

(123)

By Theorem
1.3
,
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\longrightarrow}(\mathsf{x},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
on
C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\Omega_{+})
where as usual,
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}
corresponds to the embedded process on
\Omega_{+}
. Now, by the Skorokhod
representation theorem, there exists a probability space
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Omega}},\tilde{\mathbf{P}})
on which

\tilde{\mathsf{x}}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\mathsf{x}^{(N)}
, i.e.
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\mathbf{X}^{(N)}
, and
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\Omega_{+}}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}
, i.e.
(\tilde{\mathsf{x}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}(\mathsf{x},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
,
are defined such that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s.,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{(N)}\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}(\tilde{\mathsf{x}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}),\ \ \textnormal{on}\ \ C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\Omega_{+}).

(124)

Let us fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Let us write
(\mathscr{F}_{t}^{(N)})_{t\geq 0}
for the natural filtration of

\tilde{\mathsf{x}}^{(N)}
. We first show that there exist
(\mathscr{F}_{t}^{(N)})_{t\geq 0}
-adapted independent standard Brownian motions
(\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}^{(N)})_{i=1}^{N}
so that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0
and
i=1,2,\dots,N
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(t)=x_{i}^{(N)}+\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\frac{\eta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{t}{2N}+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}s.

(125)

Let us define the process
\left(\left(\mathsf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(t)\right)_{i=1}^{N};t\geq 0\right)
coordinate-wise, as follows,

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left(\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(t)-x_{i}^{(N)}+\frac{\eta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\mathrm{d}s-\frac{t}{2N}-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}s;t\geq 0\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\mathrm{d}\mathsf{w}_{i}(s)\;t\geq 0\right).

The equality in distribution is by virtue of the fact that
\tilde{\mathsf{x}}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\mathsf{x}^{(N)}
and that
\mathsf{x}^{(N)}
is a solution to the SDE (
10
). Observe that,
\mathsf{Y}^{(N)}
is adapted with respect to
(\mathscr{F}_{t}^{(N)})_{t\geq 0}
. It is also easy to check that
\mathsf{Y}^{(N)}
is an
N
-dimensional continuous local martingale with respect to

(\mathscr{F}_{t}^{(N)})_{t\geq 0}
, with quadratic variation
23
23

23

For a continuous
N
-dimensional local martingale
(\mathsf{z}_{i}(\cdot))_{i=1}^{N}
we write
\left\langle\mathsf{z}_{i},\mathsf{z}_{j}\right\rangle_{t}
,
i,j=1,\dots,N
for its quadratic (co-)variation.

[
95
,
47
]
given by,

\left\langle\mathsf{Y}_{i}^{(N)},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(N)}\right\rangle_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{i=j}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\ i,j=1,2,\dots,N.

(126)

Now, we can define, for
i=1,\dots,N
, by virtue of the fact that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s.
\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}>0
, the stochastic integral,

\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}^{(N)}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(s),\ \ \forall t\geq 0.

(127)

Observe that,
(\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}^{(N)})_{i=1}^{N}
is adapted with respect to
(\mathscr{F}_{t}^{(N)})_{t\geq 0}
and moreover it is in fact an
N
-dimensional continuous local martingale, with respect to this filtration, satisfying,

\left\langle\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}^{(N)},\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{j}^{(N)}\right\rangle_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{i=j}t,\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\ i,j=1,2,\dots,N.

Hence, by virtue of Levy’s characterisation theorem
[
95
]
,
(\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}^{(N)})_{i=1}^{N}
is a sequence of independent standard Brownian motions. Moreover, from (
127
), we have

\mathsf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}^{(N)}(s),\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\ \ i=1,2,\dots,N,

from which (
125
) follows.

We next prove (
122
) holds.
Clearly, proving (
122
) amounts to showing the convergence of the integrals to the corresponding terms in the limit. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. We proceed to show the convergence of the drift terms first. Observe that,
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\mathrm{d}s\to\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\ \ \text{ as }N\to\infty.

We thus need to prove convergence of the interaction terms, namely,
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}s\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{\infty}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}\mathrm{d}s.

(128)

To this end, we will use Proposition
4.2
on convergence of the corresponding characteristic polynomials.
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq i\leq N
. We consider the following entire functions:

\displaystyle\Phi_{i}^{N}\left(z;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\prod_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\left(1-x_{j}^{(N)}z\right)^{2},\ \ \mathbf{x}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{W}_{N,+},

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{i}(z;\omega)

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{e}^{-2(\gamma-x_{i})z}\prod_{j=1,j\neq i}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{2x_{j}z}\left(1-x_{j}z\right)^{2},\ \ \omega=(\mathbf{x},\gamma)\in\Omega_{+}.

Observe that, the integrands in (
128
) are determined in terms of the functions
\Phi_{i}^{N}\left(z;\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)
and
\mathsf{E}_{i}(z;\omega)
respectively. Indeed,
the corresponding terms, at time
t\geq 0
, are equal to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}\log\Phi_{i}^{N}\left(z,\tilde{\mathsf{x}}^{(N)}(t)\right)\Big|_{z=\left(\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(t)\right)^{-1}},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}\log\mathsf{E}_{i}\left(z;\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\Omega_{+}}\right)\Big|_{z=\left(\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)\right)^{-1}}.

Note that, the expressions above are well-defined by virtue of the fact that the paths are
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s. non-intersecting and never hit zero. We now prove that,
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}\log\Phi_{i}^{N}\left(z,\tilde{\mathsf{x}}^{(N)}(t)\right)\Big|_{z=\left(\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(t)\right)^{-1}}\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}\log\mathsf{E}_{i}\left(z;\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\Omega_{+}}\right)\Big|_{z=\left(\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)\right)^{-1}}.

(129)

Since we have convergence on
\Omega_{+}
, as in (
124
), it follows from
Proposition
4.2
, in particular equation (
110
), that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{i}^{N}\left(z,\tilde{\mathsf{x}}^{(N)}(t)\right)\longrightarrow\mathsf{E}_{i}\left(z;\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_{t}^{\Omega_{+}}\right),\ \ \textnormal{ as }N\to\infty,

uniformly on compact sets in
\mathbb{C}
, and the same is true for the corresponding derivatives by analyticity.
Note that, by virtue of Theorem
4.3
we have
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(t)\right)^{-1}\longrightarrow\left(\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)\right)^{-1},\ \ \textnormal{as }N\to\infty.

From the above, and the fact that all processes we are considering are
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s. non-intersecting, see Lemma
3.1
and Theorem
4.3
, the convergence in (
129
) is concluded. This shows convergence of the integrands for all times
t\geq 0
