. We now prove that the corresponding integrals also converge.
First, note that using the uniform convergence and the non-intersection property of the coordinates, we have,
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0
,

\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}s\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}\mathrm{d}s.

Thus, it suffices to show that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=i+1}^{N}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}\mathrm{d}s\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}\mathrm{d}s.

Observe that, as paths are strictly ordered,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=i+1}^{N}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}\leq\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i+1}^{(N)}(s)}\sum_{j=i+1}^{N}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s),\ \ \forall s\geq 0,

where, by virtue of (
124
) and the non-intersection property of the limiting paths, the right hand side converges
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., uniformly on
[0,t]
. The desired result then follows by applying the generalized Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem.

We now deal with the stochastic integral term. From now on, let us denote by
(\mathscr{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the natural filtration of the process
\left(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\Omega_{+}}_{t};t\geq 0\right)
. We deduce from the convergence of both the LHS and the drift term in (
125
) that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathsf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(\cdot)
converges, as
N\to\infty
, uniformly on compact sets,
to the continuous process
\mathsf{Y}_{i}(\cdot)
given by,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Y}_{i}(t)=\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)-x_{i}-\int_{0}^{t}\left(-\frac{\eta}{2}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)+\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{\infty}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}\right)\mathrm{d}s,\ \ \forall t\geq 0.

Observe that,
\mathsf{Y}(\cdot)=\left(\mathsf{Y}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty}
is adapted with respect to
(\mathscr{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. We now claim that for any
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
\left(\mathsf{Y}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{K}
is a
K
-dimensional continuous local martingale with quadratic variation given by,

\langle\mathsf{Y}_{i},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\rangle_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{i=j}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\ i,j\in\mathbb{N}.

Let us assume this claim momentarily. Then, we can define, by virtue of the fact that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s.
\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}>0
, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the stochastic integral,

\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{Y}_{i}(s),\ \ \forall t\geq 0.

(130)

Observe that
\left(\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty}
is adapted with respect to
(\mathscr{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and moreover, for each
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
(\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(\cdot))_{i=1}^{K}
is a
K
-dimensional continuous local martingale with quadratic variation,

\langle\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i},\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{j}\rangle_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{i=j}t,\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\ i,j\in\mathbb{N}.

Thus, making use of Levy’s characterisation theorem
[
95
]
we get that
(\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
is a sequence of independent standard Brownian motions. Finally, from (
130
) we get, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathsf{Y}_{i}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(s),\ \ \forall t\geq 0.

This concludes the proof of (
122
) modulo the claim. We now prove it. Fix
K\in\mathbb{N}
. Define the stopping times
\zeta_{K}^{N}(R)
and
\zeta_{K}(R)
, with respect to the corresponding filtrations
(\mathscr{F}_{t}^{(N)})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\mathscr{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, for
R>0
,
N\geq K
,

\displaystyle\zeta_{K}^{N}(R)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\inf\left\{t\geq 0;\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left|\mathsf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(t)\right|\geq R\right\},\ \ \zeta_{K}(R)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\inf\left\{t\geq 0;\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left|\mathsf{Y}_{i}(t)\right|\geq R\right\}.

Observe that,
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s.,
\zeta_{K}(R)\overset{R\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty
. Moreover, arguing similarly to the proof of Theorem
4.3
we get that there exists a sequence
(R_{m})_{m=1}^{\infty}
, with
R_{m}\to\infty
so that for any fixed
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\zeta_{K}^{N}(R_{m})\longrightarrow\zeta_{K}(R_{m})
, in distribution, as
N\to\infty
. Let
t\geq s
and
R_{m}
be fixed. Let
\mathfrak{G}
be an arbitrary bounded continuous functional on
C([0,s],\Omega_{+})
, the space of continuous functions on
[0,s]
with values in
\Omega_{+}
. Note that, we have, as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{Y}^{(N)}_{i}\left(t\wedge\zeta^{N}_{K}(R_{m})\right)-\mathsf{Y}^{(N)}_{i}\left(s\wedge\zeta^{N}_{K}(R_{m})\right)\right)_{i=1}^{K}\mathfrak{G}\left[\left(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{(N)}(u);0\leq u\leq s\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\longrightarrow}\left(\mathsf{Y}_{i}\left(t\wedge\zeta_{K}(R_{m})\right)-\mathsf{Y}_{i}\left(s\wedge\zeta_{K}(R_{m})\right)\right)_{i=1}^{K}\mathfrak{G}\left[\left(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\Omega_{+}}(u);0\leq u\leq s\right)\right].

Moreover, observe that, if for fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the functional
\mathsf{G}
on
C([0,s],\mathbb{W}_{N,+})
by,

\mathsf{G}\left[\left(\left(\mathrm{f}_{i}(u)\right)_{i=1}^{N};0\leq u\leq s\right)\right]=\mathfrak{G}\left[\left(\left(\left(N^{-1}\mathrm{f}_{i}(u)\right)_{i=1}^{\infty},N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{f}_{i}(u)\right);0\leq u\leq s\right)\right],

with the convention
\mathrm{f}_{i}\equiv 0
, for
i>N
, then
\mathsf{G}
is bounded and continuous. Hence, putting everything together, by virtue of the local martingale property of
\mathsf{Y}^{(N)}
, we obtain,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\gamma)}\left[\left(\mathsf{Y}_{i}(t\wedge\zeta_{K}(R_{m}))-\mathsf{Y}_{i}(s\wedge\zeta_{K}(R_{m}))\right)_{i=1}^{K}\mathfrak{G}\left[\left(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\Omega_{+}}_{u};0\leq u\leq s\right)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}}\left[\left(\mathsf{Y}^{(N)}_{i}\left(t\wedge\zeta^{N}_{K}(R_{m})\right)-\mathsf{Y}^{(N)}_{i}\left(s\wedge\zeta^{N}_{K}(R_{m})\right)\right)_{i=1}^{K}\mathfrak{G}\left[\left(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{(N)}(u);0\leq u\leq s\right)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}^{(N)}}\left[\left(\mathsf{Y}^{(N)}_{i}\left(t\wedge\zeta^{N}_{K}(R_{m})\right)-\mathsf{Y}^{(N)}_{i}\left(s\wedge\zeta^{N}_{K}(R_{m})\right)\right)_{i=1}^{K}\mathsf{G}\left[\left(\tilde{\mathsf{x}}^{(N)}(u);0\leq u\leq s\right)\right]\right]=(0)_{i=1}^{K}.

This proves that
\left(\mathsf{Y}_{i}(\cdot)\right)_{i=1}^{K}
is a continuous local martingale, with
(\zeta_{K}(R_{m}))_{m=1}^{\infty}
a localising sequence, with respect to
\left(\mathscr{F}_{t}\right)_{t\geq 0}
. Finally note that,
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s. the quadratic variations of the
\mathsf{Y}^{(N)}
’s also converge, as
N\to\infty
, to the desired expression and this concludes the proof of the claim and thus the proof of (
122
).

We now proceed to show that
\tilde{\mathsf{x}}
actually solves (
13
). It will suffice to show that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0
, we have

\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s=0.

Observe that, for any
M\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\geq 0
, we have from (
122
),

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{M}x_{i}+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(s)-\frac{\eta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}s+M\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{j>M}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}\mathrm{d}s.

Therefore, since the first and last terms in the above display are non-negative, one has

\displaystyle M\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)-\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(s)+\frac{\eta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Note that, all the terms on the RHS are finite uniformly as
M\to\infty
. Thus, dividing both sides by
M
, and then, taking
M
to infinity, one obtains that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq 0.

But clearly,
\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s\geq 0
and thus we get
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s.,
\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s=0
, as desired.

We now want to prove the second statement in the theorem. First observe that, the above implies that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s),\ \ \textnormal{for almost all }s\in[0,t].

(131)

Furthermore, we have,
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
M\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{M}x_{i}+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(s)-\frac{\eta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{j>M}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}\mathrm{d}s.

(132)

Now, by making use of the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality for local martingales, see
[
52
,
95
]
, or directly using Problem 1.5.25 of
[
52
]
, by virtue of the fact that
\sum_{i=K}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\cdot)^{2}\to 0
, uniformly on compact sets, as
K\to\infty
, we obtain the existence of a sequence
(M_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{\infty}
with
M_{\ell}\to\infty
, as
\ell\to\infty
, such that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s. the following limit exists uniformly on compact sets,

\mathsf{U}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}(s),\ \ \forall t\geq 0.

In particular,
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s. the function
t\mapsto\mathsf{U}(t)
is continuous. Then, putting
M=M_{\ell}
and taking the limit
\ell\to\infty
in both sides of (
132
), we obtain that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}x_{i}+\mathsf{U}(t)-\frac{\eta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}\sum_{j>M_{\ell}}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}\mathrm{d}s.

(133)

Observe now that the second and third terms on the RHS of (
133
) are continuous functions of
t
, and thus, any possible discontinuity of
t\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)
arises from the last term. Note also that since the integrand is strictly positive, one has

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\mathsf{u}}\sum_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}\sum_{j>M_{\ell}}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}\mathrm{d}s>\lim_{t\nearrow\mathsf{u}}\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}\sum_{j>M_{\ell}}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}\mathrm{d}s,

at any point of discontinuity
\mathsf{u}>0
of
t\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)
, and hence, from (
133
) we get

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\mathsf{u})>\lim_{t_{k}\nearrow\mathsf{u}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t_{k})=\lim_{t_{k}\nearrow\mathsf{u}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(\mathsf{u}),

where, by virtue of (
131
), the sequence
\left(t_{k}\right)_{k=1}^{\infty}
is picked so that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t_{k})
holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and recall that
t\mapsto\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)
is continuous.
But, this is a contradiction as we know that

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)\leq\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)
, for all
t\geq 0
. Therefore, it follows that

t\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)
is, in fact, a continuous function on
(0,\infty)
and in particular, we have that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s.,

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(t)=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t),\ \ \forall t>0,

which is equivalent to (
121
). This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

It is interesting to note that Theorem
4.5
combined with Theorem
1.7
give a non-computational proof of the fact that if, instead of Definition
1.6
, we define
\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}
as the unique probability measure on
\Omega_{+}
satisfying
\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}\Lambda^{\infty}_{N}=\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}_{N}
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}
must be supported on
\Omega_{+}^{0}
. The original proof in
[
7
]
uses the underlying determinantal point process structure and some hard estimates on the correlation kernel of the Laguerre unitary ensemble. The dynamical proof we just mentioned avoids these considerations entirely.

Remark 4.7
.

We note that it is possible to obtain the convergence of the integrands in (
128
) in a slightly different (but essentially equivalent) way to the one presented in the proof of Theorem
4.5
without using characteristic polynomials. This is somewhat less intuitive but a little quicker. First, observe that, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\leq N
,
j\neq i
,

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)=\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)^{2}}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}\leq\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)^{2}}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i+1}^{(N)}(s)},\ \ \forall s\geq 0.

(134)

Next, using convergence on
\Omega_{+}
, we have that,
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s.,

\left(\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i+1}^{(N)}(s)\right)^{-1}\sum_{j=i+1}^{N}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)^{2}\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\left(\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i+1}(s)\right)^{-1}\sum_{j=i+1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)^{2},\ \ \forall s\geq 0.

From this and (
134
), making use of the generalised dominated convergence theorem for the series, we conclude that (again using convergence on
\Omega_{+}
),

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}^{(N)}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}^{(N)}(s)}=\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{\infty}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)^{2}}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s),\ \ \forall s\geq 0.

(135)

Finally, observe that we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{\infty}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)^{2}}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}=\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{\infty}\frac{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}{\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)-\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s)}-\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(s),\ \ \forall s\geq 0,

which gives what we desired to prove. In order to show convergence of the actual integrals and that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)
, for
s>0
, we use the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
4.5
.

Remark 4.8
.

Arguing as in the proof of Theorem
4.5
, we can show that, under the
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
-coupling from (
124
), for any initial condition
\omega=(\mathbf{x},\gamma)\in\Omega_{+}
(we do not need to use any non-intersection property of the paths),

\mathrm{d}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)=\mathrm{d}\mathcal{N}(t)+\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\eta}{2}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)\right)\mathrm{d}t,

(136)

where
\mathcal{N}(\cdot)
is given by the uniform limit (look at the corresponding equation for finite
N
and solve for the martingale term),

\mathcal{N}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}^{(N)}_{i}(s)\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i}^{(N)}(s),\ \forall t\geq 0,

and is in particular a one-dimensional martingale with respect to
(\mathscr{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
with quadratic variation, by virtue of the fact that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}^{(N)}_{i}(\cdot)^{2}\to\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\cdot)^{2}
uniformly on compact sets,

\langle\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)^{2}\mathrm{d}s,\ \forall t\geq 0.

Hence, there exists a one-dimensional Brownian motion
\left(\hat{\mathsf{w}}(t);t\geq 0\right)
so that the stochastic equation (
136
) can in fact be written as

\mathrm{d}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(s)^{2}}\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathsf{w}}(t)+\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\eta}{2}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)\right)\mathrm{d}t.

Finally, we note that the Brownian motion
\left(\hat{\mathsf{w}}(t);t\geq 0\right)
is dependent on the sequence of independent Brownian motions
(\tilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
driving the
(\tilde{\mathsf{x}}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
.

We now prove that solutions corresponding to different parameters
\gamma
are different in law.

Theorem 4.9
.

In the setting of Theorem
4.5
, we denote by
\left(\mathbf{X}(t;\gamma);t\geq 0\right)
the solution to (
13
) corresponding to
\gamma
, then,

\mathsf{Law}\big(\mathbf{X}(\cdot;\gamma)\big)\neq\mathsf{Law}\big(\mathbf{X}(\cdot;\tilde{\gamma})\big),\textnormal{whenever }\gamma\neq\tilde{\gamma}.

Proof.

Based on (
121
) we have that a.s.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}(t;\gamma)=\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t),\ \ \sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}(t;\tilde{\gamma})=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t),\ \ \forall t>0.

(137)

Note now that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
do not have same distribution, as they are continuous functions with
\boldsymbol{\gamma}(0)\neq\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(0)
, and thus, by virtue of (
137
),

\mathsf{Law}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}(\cdot;\gamma)\right)\neq\mathsf{Law}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}(\cdot;\tilde{\gamma})\right),

from which the conclusion follows immediately.
∎

We have the following result which completes the statement of Theorem
1.4
.

Proposition 4.10
.

Out of all solutions to the ISDE (
13
) constructed as in Theorem
4.5
, there exists a unique one such that almost surely
t\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}(t;\gamma)
is continuous for all
t\geq 0
given by the choice
\gamma=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}x_{i}
.
Moreover, this solution is a Markov process.

Proof.

We know from Theorem
1.3
that
\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}_{\cdot}=\left(\mathbf{X}(\cdot;\gamma),\boldsymbol{\gamma}(\cdot)\right)
has continuous sample paths.
It then follows from continuity of
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t)
and (
121
) that
almost surely
t\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}(t;\gamma)
is continuous for all
t\geq 0
if and only if
\gamma=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}x_{i}
.

Towards the second assertion, note that by the Markov property of
\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}
, for all

t\geq s\geq 0
and all bounded, measurable functions
\mathsf{F}:\Omega_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{F}\left(\mathbf{X}(t;\gamma),\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t)\right)\big|\mathscr{F}_{s}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{F}\left(\mathbf{X}(t;\gamma),\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t)\right)\big|\sigma\left(\mathbf{X}(s;\gamma),\boldsymbol{\gamma}(s)\right)\right],

(138)

where, as before,

\left(\mathscr{F}_{t}\right)_{t\geq 0}

is the natural filtration of the process
\left(\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}_{t};t\geq 0\right)
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\cdot|\sigma(\mathcal{C})\right]
denotes conditional expectation with respect to
\sigma(\mathcal{C})
.

Now, consider the distinguished solution
\mathbf{X}(\cdot;\gamma_{*})
of the ISDE with
\gamma_{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}x_{i}
.
Note that, since
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}(s;\gamma_{*})=\boldsymbol{\gamma}(s)
, for all
s\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma\left(\mathbf{X}\left(s;\gamma_{*}\right),\boldsymbol{\gamma}(s)\right)=\sigma\left(\mathbf{X}(s;\gamma_{*})\right),\ \ \forall s\geq 0,

(139)

and thus, in this case the natural filtration
\left(\mathscr{F}_{t}^{\mathbf{X}(\cdot;\gamma_{*})}\right)_{t\geq 0}
generated by
\left(\mathbf{X}(t;\gamma_{*});t\geq 0\right)
is equal to
\left(\mathscr{F}_{t}\right)_{t\geq 0}
. Denote by
\mathcal{W}^{\infty}
the state space of the solution:

\mathcal{W}^{\infty}=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{\infty,+}^{\circ}:\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}x_{i}<\infty\right\}.

Now, let
\mathsf{f}:\mathcal{W}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
be an arbitrary bounded measurable function and take
\mathsf{F}
to be its obvious lift on
\Omega_{+}
. Thus summarising, by virtue (
138
) and (
139
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{f}\left(\mathbf{X}(t;\gamma_{*})\right)\big|\mathscr{F}_{s}^{\mathbf{X}(\cdot;\gamma_{*})}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{f}\left(\mathbf{X}(t;\gamma_{*})\right)\big|\sigma\left(\mathbf{X}(s;\gamma_{*})\right)\right],\ \ \forall s\geq 0,

which implies that
\left(\mathbf{X}\left(t;\gamma_{*}\right)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is a Markov process and concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

This follows from Theorem
4.3
, Theorem
4.5
, Theorem
4.9
, and Proposition
4.10
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

By Theorem
2.22
, the probability measure
\mathfrak{m}
corresponds to the unique sequence of probability measures
\left(\mathfrak{m}_{N}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
on
\left(\mathbb{W}_{N,+}\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
given by,

\mathfrak{m}_{N}=\mathfrak{m}\Lambda_{N}^{\infty},\ \ \forall N\in\mathbb{N}.

Let
\mathsf{x}^{(N)}
be the solution of (
10
) started at
\mathsf{x}^{(N)}(0)\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\mathfrak{m}_{N}
. By combining Theorem
2.22
and Lemma
2.4
, a standard argument gives that, under the embedding (
41
) of
\mathbb{W}_{N,+}
into
\Omega_{+}
,
\mathfrak{m}_{N}
converges weakly to
\mathfrak{m}
. Namely,
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}_{0}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}_{0}
, and hence,

\mathbf{X}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}
by Theorem
2.31
. One can then follow the argument of Theorem
4.5
to prove that
\left(\mathbf{X}(t;\mathfrak{m});t\geq 0\right)
is a weak solution of the ISDE (
13
) with initial condition
\mathbf{X}(0;\mathfrak{m})\overset{\textnormal{d}}{=}\mathsf{IBes}_{\eta}
. Note that, as
\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}
is the invariant measure of
\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}
, we have

\mathsf{Law}\big(\mathbf{X}(t;\mathfrak{M}^{\eta})\big)=\mathsf{IBes}_{\eta},\ \ \forall t\geq 0,

On the other hand, if
\mathfrak{m}\neq\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}
, we must have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathfrak{m}}\left(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(0;\mathfrak{m})>\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}(0;\mathfrak{m})\right)>0,

where
\mathbb{P}_{\mathfrak{m}}
denotes
\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathbf{X}^{\Omega_{+}}\right)
if
\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\Omega_{+}}\right)=\mathfrak{m}
. Thus, based on the arguments in Theorem
4.9
, since
\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t;\mathfrak{m})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}(t;\mathfrak{m})
for all
t>0
, and recall that
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{\gamma}(t;\mathfrak{m})
is continuous, we obtain that the stochastic processes
t\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}(t;\mathfrak{m})
and
t\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{x}_{i}(t;\mathfrak{M}^{\eta})
are not equal in law, for any
\mathfrak{m}\neq\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}
, and so,

\mathsf{Law}\big(\mathbf{X}\left(\cdot;\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}\right)\big)\neq\mathsf{Law}\big(\mathbf{X}\left(\cdot;\mathfrak{m}\right)\big).

That is, the following holds if and only if
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{M}^{\eta}
,

\mathsf{Law}\big(\mathbf{X}\left(t;\mathfrak{m}\right)\big)=\mathsf{IBes}_{\eta},\ \ \forall t\geq 0,

and this completes the proof.
∎

5

Dynamical Cauchy model

In this section, we consider stochastic dynamics related to the Cauchy
[
113
,
38
]
or so-called Hua-Pickrell ensemble
[
16
]
. This is the following probability measure on
\mathbb{W}_{N}
,
depending on a parameter
s\in\mathbb{C}
, with
\Re(s)>-\frac{1}{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}_{N}^{(s)}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{(s)}}\Delta_{N}^{2}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{N}\left(1+x_{j}^{2}\right)^{-\Re({s})-N}\mathrm{e}^{2\Im({s})\tan^{-1}(x_{j})}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},

where,

\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{(s)}
is a normalizing constant.

The associated
N
-particle dynamics, that we call the dynamical Cauchy model, introduced in
[
8
]
(they were Hua-Pickrell diffusions therein), is given by the unique solution to the following system of stochastic equations

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{x}_{i}(t)=\sqrt{2\left[\mathfrak{x}_{i}(t)^{2}+1\right]}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{w}_{i}(t)+2\left[\left(1-N-\Re({s})\right)\mathfrak{x}_{i}(t)+\Im({s})\right]\mathrm{d}t+2\sum_{j=1:j\neq i}^{N}\frac{\mathfrak{x}_{i}(t)^{2}+1}{\mathfrak{x}_{i}(t)-\mathfrak{x}_{j}(t)}\mathrm{d}t,

(140)

for
i=1,\dots,N
, where as usual the
\mathsf{w}_{i}
are independent standard Brownian motions.
It is proven in
[
8
]
, making use of the results of
[
45
]
, that
(
140
) has a unique strong solution for any initial condition
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
and almost surely for all
t>0
,
\mathsf{x}(t)\in\mathbb{W}_{N}^{\circ}
and moreover the associated semigroup
\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{(s)}_{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is Feller. The following result is the main theorem of
[
8
]
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{C}
. Then, the semigroups
\left(\mathfrak{Q}_{N}^{(s)}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
are intertwined:

\mathfrak{Q}_{N+1}^{(s)}(t)\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}=\Lambda_{N}^{N+1}\mathfrak{Q}_{N}^{(s)}(t),\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\ N\in\mathbb{N}.

In particular, there exists a unique Feller semigroup
\left(\mathfrak{Q}_{\infty}^{(s)}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
, with associated stochastic process
\left(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\Omega};t\geq 0\right)
on
\Omega
, defined via the intertwining

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{\infty}^{(s)}(t)\Lambda_{N}^{\infty}=\Lambda_{N}^{\infty}\mathfrak{Q}_{N}^{(s)}(t),\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\ N\in\mathbb{N}.

Moreover, if
\Re(s)>-\frac{1}{2}
, then
\left(\mathfrak{Q}_{\infty}^{(s)}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
has a unique invariant measure
\mathfrak{N}_{\infty}^{(s)}
uniquely determined via the relation

\mathfrak{N}_{\infty}^{(s)}\Lambda_{N}^{\infty}=\mathfrak{N}_{N}^{(s)},\ \ \forall N\in\mathbb{N}.

Before giving our main result on the dynamical Cauchy model it is worth pointing out, that for real
s>-\frac{1}{2}
, the invariant measure associated to the dynamics
\left(\mathfrak{Q}^{s}_{\infty}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is known to admit an explicit description. We briefly spell this out.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
s>-\frac{1}{2}
. The Hua-Pickrell point process
\mathsf{HP}^{\textnormal{Conf}}_{s}
with parameter
s
is the determinantal point process on
(-\infty,0)\times(0,\infty)
with correlation kernel
\mathfrak{K}_{s}
given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{s}(z,w)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{\Gamma(s+1)^{2}}{\Gamma(2s+1)\Gamma(2s+2)}\frac{\mathcal{Y}_{1}^{(s)}(z)\mathcal{Y}_{2}^{(s)}(w)-\mathcal{Y}_{1}^{(s)}(w)\mathcal{Y}_{2}^{(s)}(z)}{z-w},

where the functions
\mathcal{Y}_{1}^{(s)}(z),\mathcal{Y}_{2}^{(s)}(z)
are given by

\mathcal{Y}_{1}^{(s)}(z)=2^{2s-\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma\left(s+\frac{1}{2}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{|z|^{\frac{1}{2}}}J_{s-1/2}\left(\frac{1}{|z|}\right),\ \ \mathcal{Y}_{2}^{(s)}(z)=2^{2s+\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma\left(s+\frac{3}{2}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{|z|^{\frac{1}{2}}}J_{s+1/2}\left(\frac{1}{|z|}\right),

where as before
J_{r}
denotes the Bessel function with parameter
r
.

For
s=0
and under the transformation
x\mapsto x^{-1}
,
\mathsf{HP}^{\textnormal{Conf}}_{0}
becomes the sine determinantal point process, the universal limit of random matrices in the bulk of the spectrum
[
38
]
. Since
\mathsf{HP}^{\textnormal{Conf}}_{s}
consists of distinct points it gives rise to a unique probability measure
\mathsf{HP}_{s}
on
\mathbb{W}_{\infty,+}^{\circ}\times\mathbb{W}_{\infty,+}
by labelling its positive and negative points respectively according to their absolute values. Define the space

\displaystyle\Omega^{0}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{\omega=\left(\mathbf{x}^{+},\mathbf{x}^{-},\gamma,\delta\right)\in\Omega:\gamma=\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left[x_{i}^{+}\mathbf{1}_{x_{i}^{+}\geq k^{-2}}-x_{i}^{-}\mathbf{1}_{x_{i}^{-}\geq k^{-2}}\right],\delta=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left[\left(x^{+}_{i}\right)^{2}+\left(x^{-}_{i}\right)^{2}\right]\right\},

and write
\pi:\Omega\to\mathbb{W}_{\infty,+}\times\mathbb{W}_{\infty,+}
for the map
\pi((\mathbf{x}^{+},\mathbf{x}^{-},\gamma,\delta))=(\mathbf{x}^{+},\mathbf{x}^{-})
. Then, it turns out that
\mathfrak{N}_{\infty}^{(s)}
is the unique probability measure on
\Omega
which is supported on
\Omega^{0}
and moreover satisfies (see the discussion in
[
8
]
and the references therein),

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\mathfrak{N}^{(s)}_{\infty}=\mathsf{HP}_{s}.

We now upgrade the abstract construction of
\left(\mathbf{X}^{\Omega}_{t};t\geq 0\right)
from Theorem
5.1
to a strong approximation theorem from the finite dimensional dynamics which moreover implies that
\left(\mathbf{X}^{\Omega}_{t};t\geq 0\right)
has continuous sample paths. Finally, we prove convergence to equilibrium from any initial condition. We leave the very interesting problem of constructing an ISDE, the drift of which will most likely require renormalisation to even make sense, to future work. We define
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}=\left(\left(\mathsf{x}^{(N),+}_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{\infty},\left(\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N),-}\right)_{i=1}^{\infty},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(N)},\boldsymbol{\delta}^{(N)}\right)
to be the process on
\Omega
obtained from (
140
) under the embedding (
40
) of
\mathbb{W}_{N}
into
\Omega
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{C}
. Consider the Feller process
\left(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\Omega};t\geq 0\right)
with semigroup
\left(\mathfrak{Q}_{\infty}^{(s)}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
constructed in Theorem
5.1
and the embedded processes
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}
on
\Omega
defined above. Let
\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\Omega}=\omega\in\Omega
be arbitrary and assume
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}(0)\to\omega
in the topology of
\Omega
. Then, as
N\to\infty
,

\mathbf{X}^{(N)}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{X}^{\Omega},\ \ \textnormal{in}\ \ C(\mathbb{R}_{+},\Omega).

Moreover, there exists a coupling of the
\mathbf{X}^{(N)}
and
\mathbf{X}^{\Omega}
such that, almost surely, for all
T\geq 0
, we have:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N),+}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{i}^{+}(t)\right|^{3}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|\mathsf{x}_{i}^{(N),-}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-}(t)\right|^{3}\right)\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

Finally, for
\Re{(s)}>-\frac{1}{2}
and
\mathfrak{K}
an arbitrary probability measure on
\Omega
, if
\mathsf{Law}\left(\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\Omega}\right)=\mathfrak{K}
, then as
t\to\infty
,

\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\Omega}\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{Z},\ \ \textnormal{where }\mathsf{Law}(\mathbf{Z})=\mathfrak{N}_{\infty}^{s}.

Proof.

All the statements in the theorem, except for convergence to equilibrium, follow by combining Theorem
5.1
and the fact that
C^{\infty}_{c,\textnormal{sym}}(\mathbb{W}_{N})
is a core, and invariant under the generator of
(\mathfrak{Q}_{N}^{(s)}(t))_{t\geq 0}
(this can be proven in exactly the same way as Proposition
3.7
), by virtue of Theorem
2.30
and Proposition
2.34
. The only thing that remains to note is convergence to equilibrium of the finite-dimensional processes which itself follows from that of the corresponding matrix process shown in
[
6
]
. Then, the remaining statement in the theorem follows from Theorem
2.10
.
∎
